Pełczyński’s type sets and Pełczyński’s geometrical properties of locally convex spaces
Saak Gabriyelyan

Abstract
For 1 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ ∞ 1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty and a locally convex space E E , we introduce and study the ( V ∗ ) (V^{\ast}) subsets of order ( p , q ) (p,q) of E E and the ( V ) (V) subsets of order ( p , q ) (p,q) of the topological dual E ′ E^{\prime} of E E . Using these sets we define and study the (sequential) Pełczyński’s property V ∗ V^{\ast} of order ( p , q ) (p,q) , the (sequential) Pełczyński’s property V V of order ( p , q ) (p,q) , and the Pełczyński’s property ( u ) (u) of order p p in the class of all locally convex spaces. To this end, we also introduce and study several new completeness type properties, weak barrelledness conditions, Schur type properties, the Gantmacher property for locally convex spaces, and ( q , p ) (q,p) -summing operators between locally convex spaces. Applications to some classical function spaces are given.



2010 MSC
46A03, 46A08, 46E10

1
Introduction

It follows from a result of Orlicz
[
68
]
that every weakly compact operator between Banach spaces sends every weakly unconditionally Cauchy (wuC) sequence into an unconditionally convergent (u.c.) series. Recall that a series
\sum_{n\in\omega}x_{n}
in a Banach space
X
is called
wuC
(resp.,
u.c.
) if for every real null (resp., bounded) sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
, the series
\sum_{n\in\omega}t_{n}x_{n}
is convergent. Later in
[
70
]
, Pełczyński proved that every operator from a Banach space
C(K)
into a Banach space no subspace of which is isomorphic to
c_{0}
is weakly compact. A complete explanation of these results was given by Pełczyński in his fundamental article
[
71
]
. Following
[
71
]
, an operator
T:X\to Y
between Banach spaces
X
and
Y
is called
unconditionally converging
(u.c.) if it sends wuC series in
X
into u.c. series in
Y
. Then, for a Banach space
X
, the following conditions are equivalent: (a) for every Banach space
Y
, every u.c. operator
T:X\to Y
is weakly compact, (b) each subset
B
of the Banach dual
X^{\prime}
satisfying the condition

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup\{|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|:\chi\in B\}=0

for every wuC series
\sum_{n}x_{n}
in
X
, is sequentially compact in the weak topology of
X^{\prime}
. This amazing result motivates to introduce the following classes of Banach spaces.

Definition 1.1
(
[
71
]
)

A Banach space
X
is said to have

\bullet

the property
V
if each subset
B
of the Banach dual
X^{\prime}
satisfying the condition

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup\{|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|:\chi\in B\}=0

for every wuC series
\sum_{n}x_{n}
in
X
, is a weakly sequentially compact subset of
X^{\prime}
;

\bullet

the property
V^{\ast}
if each subset
A
of
X
satisfying the condition

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup\{|\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle|:x\in A\}=0

(1.1)

for every wuC series
\sum_{n}\chi_{n}
in
X^{\prime}
, is weakly sequentially compact.∎

The following remarkable assertion was proved by Pełczyński.

Theorem 1.2
(
[
71
]
)

For every compact space
K
, the Banach space
C(K)
has the property
V
.

As a corollary he proved that every abstract
L
-space has the property
(V^{\ast})
. It should be mentioned that a Banach space is reflexive if and only if it has both properties
(V)
and
(V^{\ast})
, see Proposition 7 in
[
71
]
.

Since numerous important results were obtained by many authors. A characterization of Banach spaces with the property
V^{\ast}
was obtained by Emmanuele
[
25
]
. Randrianantoanina
[
77
]
extended Theorem
1.2
by showing that if
K
is a compact space and
E
is a separable Banach space, then the Banach space
C(K,E)
has the property
V
if and only if
E
has the property
V
. Pfitzner proved in
[
74
]
that every
C^{\ast}
-algebra has the property
V
. In
[
46
]
, Godefroy and Saab showed that any Banach space which is an
M
-ideal in its bidual has the property
V
. Saab and Saab proved in
[
81
]
that a Banach lattice
X
has the property
V^{\ast}
if and only if
X
contains no subspaces isomorphic to
c_{0}
. Answering a problem of Godefroy and Saab
[
45
]
, Castillo and González showed in
[
10
]
that the property
V
is not a three-space property. It turns out that a lot of important concrete Banach spaces have the property
V
, see for example Bourgain
[
9
]
, Delbaen
[
22
]
and Kislyakov
[
56
]
. Johnson and Zippin showed in
[
53
]
that all real preduals have the property
V
. Chu and Mellon proved in
[
18
]
that all
JB^{\ast}
-triples have the property
V
.

In
[
8
]
, Bombal introduced the weak version of the property
V^{\ast}
: A Banach space
X
has the
weak
V^{\ast}
if each subset
A
of
X
satisfying the condition (
1.1
) is weakly sequentially precompact. A characterization of Banach spaces with the weak
V^{\ast}
was given by Saab and Saab in
[
81
, p. 530]
. An operator characterization of
V
-sets and sufficient and necessary conditions to have the property
V
were obtained recently by Cilia and Emmanuele
[
19
]
.

An important tool in the study of subspaces of Banach spaces and Banach spaces with unconditional bases is Pełczyński’s property
(u)
.

Definition 1.3
(
[
69
]
)

A Banach space
X
is said to have the property
(u)
if, for every weakly Cauchy sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
X
, there exists a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
X
such that

(i)

the series
\sum_{n\in\omega}x_{n}
is weakly unconditionally convergent, i.e.
\sum_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|<\infty
for every
\chi\in X^{\prime}
,

(ii)

the sequence
\{y_{n}-\sum_{j\leq n}x_{j}\}_{n\in\omega}
is weakly null. ∎

In
[
69
]
Pełczyński proved that every closed subspace of a Banach space with an unconditional basis has the property
(u)
. For Banach lattices a similar result was proved by Tzafriri
[
82
]
: any order continuous Banach lattice
E
has the property
(u)
. It follows from a result of Karlin
[
55
]
that the Banach space
C[0,1]
cannot be embedded in a space with unconditional basis. In
[
44
]
, Godefroy and Li proved that Banach spaces which are
M
-ideals in their bidual have the property
(u)
. Castillo and González showed in
[
10
]
that the property
(u)
is not a three-space property. There is a natural connection between the property
(u)
and the property
V
found by Pełczyński
[
71
]
: If a Banach space
X
has the property
(u)
and has no isomorphic copy of
\ell_{1}
, then
X
has the property
V
. Cembranos, Kalton, Saab and Saab proved in
[
15
]
that for every compact space
K
and each Banach space
X
which does not contain an isomorphic copy of
\ell_{1}
and with the property
(u)
, the space
C(K,X)
has the property
V
. In
[
79
]
, Rosenthal proved that a Banach space
X
has the property
(u)
and has no isomorphic copy of
\ell_{1}
if and only if each closed subspace of
X
has the property
V
.

For other important results related to Pełczyński’s properties
V
,
V^{\ast}
and
(u)
, we refer the reader to the articles
[
8
,
15
,
17
,
19
,
25
,
26
,
46
,
73
,
77
,
78
,
80
]
and the classical books
[
1
,
11
,
24
,
50
,
62
,
63
]
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
,
E
be a locally convex space (lcs for short), and let
E^{\prime}
be the topological dual of
E
. Recall (see Section 19.4 in
[
52
]
and
[
14
]
) that a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
is called
weakly
p
-summable
or a
weak
\ell_{p}
-sequence
if for every
\chi\in E^{\prime}
it follows that
(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle)\in\ell_{p}
if
p\in[1,\infty)
and
(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle)\in c_{0}
if
p=\infty
.
The family of all weakly
p
summable sequences in
E
is denoted by
\ell_{p}^{w}(E)
or
c_{0}^{w}(E)
if
p=\infty
.

Unifying the notion of u.c. operator and the notion of completely continuous operators (i.e., they transform weakly null sequences into norm null), Castillo and Sánchez selected in
[
12
]
the class of
p
-convergent operators. An operator
T:X\to Y
between Banach spaces is called
p
-convergent
if it transforms weakly
p
-summable sequences into norm null sequences. Using this notion they introduced and study Banach spaces with the Dunford–Pettis property of order
p
(
DPP_{p}
for short) for every
p\in[1,\infty]
. A Banach space
X
is said to have the
DPP_{p}
if every weakly compact operator from
X
into a Banach space
Y
is
p
-convergent.

The influential article of Castillo and Sánchez
[
12
]
inspired an intensive study of
p
-versions of numerous geometrical properties of Banach spaces, in particular, Pełczyński’s properties
V
and
V^{\ast}
of order
p

[
16
,
61
]
, the Gelfand–Phillips property of order
p

[
42
]
, the Schur property of order
p

[
21
,
32
]
and others. Let us recall the definitions of
V^{\ast}
sets of order
p
and
V
sets of order
p
which were defined and studied by Chen, Chávez-Domínguez, and Li in
[
61
]
and
[
16
]
, respectively.

Definition 1.4

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
,
X
be a Banach space, and let
A
and
B
be bounded subsets of
X
and the Banach dual
X^{\prime}
of
X
, respectively. Then:

(i)

A
is called a
p
-
(V^{\ast})
set if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{a\in A}|\langle\chi_{n},a\rangle|=0\;\;\mbox{ for every $(\chi_{n})\in\ell_{p}^{w}(X^{\prime})$ \big(or $(\chi_{n})\in c_{0}^{w}(X^{\prime})$ if $p=\infty$\big);}

(ii)

B
is called a
p
-
(V)
set if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\chi\in B}|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|=0\;\;\mbox{ for every $(x_{n})\in\ell_{p}^{w}(X)$ \big(or $(x_{n})\in c_{0}^{w}(X)$ if $p=\infty$\big).\qed}

The notion of
p
-
(V)
sets was applied in
[
16
]
to characterize
DPP_{p}
. Being motivated by Pełczyński’s properties
V
and
V^{\ast}
, one can naturally define (to unify both notions, for the property
V
of order
p
we use an equivalent definition given in Theorem 2.4 of
[
61
]
)

Definition 1.5
(
[
61
]
)

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
. A Banach space
X
is said to have

(i)

Pełczyński’s property
V
of order
p
if every
p
-
(V)
subset of
X^{\prime}
is relatively weakly compact;

(ii)

Pełczyński’s property
V^{\ast}
of order
p
if every
p
-
(V^{\ast})
subset of
X
is relatively weakly compact.

Chen, Chávez-Domínguez, and Li proved in
[
61
]
that for every
1<p<\infty
, the James
p
-space
J_{p}
has Pełczyński’s property
V^{\ast}
of order
p
and the Pełczyński’s property
V
of order
p^{\ast}
, where
p^{\ast}
is the conjugate number of
p
. However, since
J_{p}
does not have the property
V
it follows that Pełczyński’s properties
V
of order
p
are indeed depend on
p
. They also defined a quantitative version of the property
V^{\ast}
. For
p\in[1,\infty)
, the
p
-version of the weak
V^{\ast}
property was introduced and thoroughly studied by Ghenciu
[
42
]
. It is worth mentioning that a quantitative version of the property
V
was defined and studied by Krulišová
[
58
,
59
]
.

The aforementioned results motivate to introduce and study analogous type of sets and properties in the general theory of locally convex spaces. This is the main goal of the article.

Now we describe the content of the article. In Section
2
we fix basic notions and prove some necessary results used in the article. Especially this is important for compact type sets as, for example, (pre)compact sets or sequentially (pre)compact sets. Indeed, it is well known that a Banach space
X
is weakly angelic, and hence (relatively) weakly compact sets are exactly (relatively) weakly sequentially compact sets. By this reason, in Banach space theory, weakly sequentially compact sets are considered usually as weakly compact. However, if
E
is a locally convex space which is not weakly angelic, these notions are completely different. Moreover, even countable precompact sets can be not sequentially precompact, see Lemma
2.2
. Therefore it seems reasonable to distinguish “compact” and “sequentially compact” constructions as it is done by Pełczyński in Definition
1.1
.
We note also that some of results of Section
2
are of independent interest, see for example Propositions
2.3
and
2.4
.

The classical notions of being a quasi-complete, sequentially complete or locally complete space will play a considerable role in the article. It should be mentioned that the study of completeness type properties of locally convex spaces is an important area of the theory of locally convex spaces, we refer the reader to the classical books
[
52
]
,
[
72
]
and
[
83
]
and references therein.
It turns out that to obtain some of our main results some new completeness type properties (which lie between the quasi-completeness and the sequential completeness) appear naturally. By this reason we separate a new section, Section
3
, in which we recall classical and define new completeness type properties. Applications to the space
C_{p}(X)
of continuous functions on a Tychonoff space
X
endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence are given. It is well known that
C_{p}(X)
is quasi-complete if and only if
X
is discrete, see Theorem 3.6.6 of
[
52
]
. In Theorem
3.13
we generalize this result by showing that the space
C_{p}(X)
is von Neumann complete if and only if
X
is discrete.
The Buchwalter–Schmets theorem states that
C_{p}(X)
is sequentially complete if and only if
X
is a
P
-space. Recall that a Tychonoff space is called a
P
-space
if any countable intersection of open sets is open. Theorem 1.1 of
[
30
]
generalizes this result: the space
C_{p}(X)
is locally complete if and only if
X
is a
P
-space.
In Theorem
3.15
below we strengthen both these results and give an independent and simpler proof of the second one (using a functional characterization of
P
-spaces given in Proposition
3.14
):
C_{p}(X)
is separably quasi-complete if and only if
X
is a
P
-space.

In Section
4
we recall the definitions of weakly
p
-summable, weakly
p
-convergent and weakly
p
-Cauchy sequences in locally convex spaces. The families
\ell_{p}^{w}(E)
and
c_{0}^{w}(E)
of all weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
endowed with natural locally convex vector topologies are locally convex spaces denoted by
\ell_{p}[E]
and
c_{0}[E]
. We recall in detail these topologies and extend Proposition 19.4.2 of
[
52
]
(which states that if
E
is complete, then the spaces
\ell_{p}[E]
and
c_{0}[E]
are complete as well), see Proposition
4.11
.
If
X
is a Banach space, the well-known result of Grothendieck
[
49
]
(see also Proposition 2.2 in
[
24
]
) states that there is the canonical isomorphism between the families
\mathcal{L}(\ell_{p^{\ast}},X)
of all operators from
\ell_{p^{\ast}}
into
X
and
\ell_{p}^{w}(X)
defined by
T\mapsto\{T(e_{n})\}_{n\in\omega}
(if
p=1
or
p=\infty
, then between
\mathcal{L}(c_{0},X)
and
\ell_{1}^{w}(X)
and, respectively, between
\mathcal{L}(\ell_{1},X)
and
\ell_{\infty}(X)
). In general, an analogous result does not hold because an lcs
E
can be not complete. Therefore to get a similar correspondence, instead of
\ell_{p}
and
c_{0}
, we consider (dense) linear spans
\ell_{p}^{0}
and
c_{0}^{0}
of the canonical basis, see Proposition
4.14
.
Similarly, for a Banach space
X
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, the map
\mathcal{L}(X,\ell_{p})\to\ell_{p}^{w}(X^{\ast}),T\mapsto\{T^{\ast}(e^{\ast}_{n})\}_{n\in\omega}
, is an isomorphism playing an essential role in the study of Pełczyński’s properties of order
p
. However, in the general case of locally convex spaces an analogous result is satisfied if an lcs
E
is barrelled, see Proposition
4.19
. A necessary condition on an lcs
E
, under which the map
T\mapsto\{T^{\ast}(e^{\ast}_{n})\}_{n\in\omega}
is an isomorphism, is the property of being a
p
-(quasi)barrelled space, see Proposition
4.17
. An lcs
E
is called
p
-(quasi)barrelled
if every weakly
p
-summable sequence in
(E,\sigma(E^{\prime},E))
(resp., in
(E,\beta(E^{\prime},E))
) is equicontinuous.

Taking into account that
p
-(quasi)barrelled spaces play a considerable role in our article we study this class of spaces in Section
5
being motivated also by the fact that weak barrelledness concepts are the cornerstone in the study of general locally convex spaces, see for example the classical books
[
52
,
57
,
72
]
. In Example
5.4
we show that there exist metrizable spaces which are not
p
-barrelled, and Example
5.5
shows that there are
c_{0}
-quasibarrelled spaces which are not
p
-quasibarrelled. Therefore the
p
-(quasi)barrelledness is indeed a new weak barrelledness notion, and hence to find conditions on an lcs
E
under which this new concept coincides with the classical notion of
c_{0}
-(quasi)barrelled spaces is an interesting problem. We show in Corollary
5.7
that if
E
is a Mackey space, then
E
is
c_{0}
-barrelled if and only if it is
p
-barrelled for some (every)
p\in[1,\infty]
. In Proposition
5.8
we show that the class of
p
-(quasi)barrelled spaces is stable under taking quotients, products, countable locally convex direct sums and completions (of large subspaces).

A property of being a Schur space is important in the study of Pełczyński’s properties. Locally convex spaces with the Schur property are thoroughly studied in
[
35
]
. For
p\in[1,\infty]
, Banach spaces with the
p
-Schur property was defined by Deghani and Moshtaghioun
[
21
]
and Fourie and Zeekoei
[
32
]
: a Banach space
E
has the
p
-Schur property
(or
E\in C_{p}
, see
[
12
]
) if every weakly
p
-summable sequence is a norm null-sequence. In Section
6
, we introduce and briefly study locally convex spaces with the
p
-Schur property. Considering the spaces
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{p}^{0}
we show that the property of being a
p
-Schur space is different for distinct
p
, see Proposition
6.5
.

In Section
7
, for
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, we introduce and study
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
and
(p,q)
-
(EV^{\ast})
sets in a locally convex space
E
(where the letter ‘
E
’ means ‘equicontinuous’). If
E
is a Banach space,
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets are exactly
p
-
(V^{\ast})
defined in Definition
1.4
. To consider the double index
(p,q)
is motivated not only but its natural generalization of one index
p
-
(V^{\ast})
sets, but also by the fact that if
1<p<\infty
, then
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets of a Banach space
X
are exactly coarse
p
-limited sets introduced and studied by Galindo and Miranda
[
41
]
(this fact immediately follows from Theorem
14.16
, for more details see
[
39
]
). Therefore considering double index we unify the study of some important classes of bounded sets. Moreover, the
(q,p)
-summing operators between Banach spaces give another strong motivation to consider double index
(p,q)
, see Sections
16
and
17
.
On the other hand, the reason to introduce the ‘equicontinuous’ version of
(V^{\ast})
-type sets is motivated by the non-uniqueness of extension of these sets from the case of Banach spaces to the general case of locally convex spaces. Indeed, if
X
is a Banach space (or, more generally,
X
is a
p
-quasibarrelled space), then every sequence
(\chi_{n})\in\ell_{p}^{w}(X^{\prime})
is automatically equicontinuous. Therefore generalizing the notion of
(V^{\ast})
sets from the Banach case to the general case of locally convex spaces we can assume that the sequence
(\chi_{n})
satisfies one of the following conditions: (1) it is simply weakly null in the strong dual
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, or (2) it is weakly null in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
and additionally equicontinuous. For example, to define limited sets in a locally convex space
E
the first condition (for weak
∗
null sequences) was used in our article
[
6
]
and the second one was used by Lindström and Schlumprecht
[
64
]
. The classes of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets and
(p,q)
-
(EV^{\ast})
sets are saturated, closed under taking images and well-behaved under sums and products, see Lemma
7.2
and Proposition
7.4
. For every
p\in[1,\infty]
, each precompact subset
A
of
E
is a
p
-
(EV^{\ast})
set and if
E
is
p
-quasibarrelled, then every precompact subset
A
of
E
is a
p
-
(V^{\ast})
set, see Proposition
7.6
.
In Theorem
7.8
we show that for every
p\in[1,\infty]
, every bounded subset of an lcs
E
is a
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
set if and only if
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
has the
p
-Schur property. In Theorem
7.13
we give an operator characterization of locally convex spaces whose
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
set are precompact.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
. In Section
8
we introduce and study
(p,q)
-
(V)
and
(p,q)
-
(EV)
sets in the topological dual
E^{\prime}
of an lcs
E
. We show in Lemma
8.2
and Proposition
8.4
that the classes of
(p,q)
-
(V)
and
(p,q)
-
(EV)
sets are saturated, closed under taking images and well-behaved under sums and products. Also we introduce and study weak
∗

(p,q)
-
(V)
sets and weak
∗

(p,q)
-
(EV)
sets in
E^{\prime}
, see Lemma
8.6
. In Example
8.8
we show that these classes are different in general.

Let
E
be a locally convex space. Any family of subsets in
E^{\prime}
, in particular the family of all
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets, naturally defines weak barrelledness conditions. For example,
E
is said to be
V_{(p,q)}
-
(
quasi
)
barrelled
if every (resp., strongly bounded) set
B
in
E^{\prime}
such that
B
is a
(p,q)
-
(V)
set is equicontinuous. In Corollary
9.5
we show that for every Tychonoff space
X
, the space
C_{p}(X)
is
V_{(p,q)}
-barrelled. Consequently, if
X
contains an infinite functionally bounded subset, then the space
C_{p}(X)
is
V_{(p,q)}
-barrelled but not
c_{0}
-barrelled. The class of locally convex spaces with such weak barrelledness conditions is closed under taking quotients, products, countable locally convex direct sums and completions, see Proposition
9.6
.

In Section
10
we introduce
V
type properties of locally convex spaces. For
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, an lcs
E
is said to have a
property
V_{(p,q)}
(a
property
sV_{(p,q)}
or a
weak property
sV_{(p,q)}
) if every
(p,q)
-
(V)
set in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is relatively weakly compact (resp., relatively weakly sequentially compact or weakly sequentially precompact). Equicontinuous versions of these properties are also defined. Taking into account that Banach spaces with the weak topology are angelic, it is clear that the classical notion of the property
V
and and the property
V_{p}
coincide with the property
sV_{(1,\infty)}
and the property
sV_{(p,\infty)}
, respectively. Permanent properties and relationships between
V
type properties are studied. In particular, we show that the class of spaces with these properties are closed under taking products, (countable) direct sums and completions, see Lemma
10.2
and Propositions
10.4
and
10.8
. If
H
is a closed subspace of
E
with one of the
V
type properties such that the quotient map
q:E\to E/H
is almost bounded-covering, then the quotient space
E/H
has the same property, see Proposition
10.5
. This result generalizes Corollary 1 of
[
71
]
which states that a quotient space of a Banach space with the property
V
has the property
V
, as well. In Theorem
10.10
we show that for every Tychonoff space
X
, the space
C_{p}(X)
has all
V
type properties. At the end of this section we remark that in the realm of Banach spaces, the property
sV_{(p,\infty)}
(resp.,
wsV_{(p,\infty)}
) coincides with the (resp., weak) reciprocal Dunford–Pettis property of order
p
introduced and studied by Ghenciu
[
42
]
.

In Section
11
we define
V^{\ast}
type properties as follows: if
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, an lcs
E
is said to have a
property
V^{\ast}_{(p,q)}
(a
property
sV^{\ast}_{(p,q)}
or a
weak property
sV_{(p,q)}^{\ast}
) if every
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
set in
E
is relatively weakly compact (resp., relatively weakly sequentially compact or weakly sequentially precompact). Analogously we define equicontinuous versions of these properties. Therefore, if
E
is a Banach space and
q=\infty
we obtain the property
V^{\ast}_{p}
and its weak version mentioned above (see
[
8
]
and
[
42
]
). In Lemma
11.2
, Propositions
11.3
and
11.7
we study categorical properties and relationships between different types of
V^{\ast}
type properties. In Proposition 7 of
[
71
]
Pełczyński proved that a Banach space
X
is reflexive if and only if it has both properties
V
and
V^{\ast}
. In Proposition
11.4
we obtain partial extensions of this result to any locally convex space.
In Corollary
11.11
we prove that if
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
X
is a Tychonoff space, then
C_{p}(X)
has the property
V^{\ast}_{(p,q)}
if and only if
X
is discrete. We remark that for Banach spaces, the property
sV^{\ast}_{p}
is nothing else than the reciprocal Dunford-Pettis
∗
property of order
p
introduced by Bator, Lewis and Ochoa
[
7
]
.

In Section
12
we introduce and study locally convex spaces
E
with the property
p
-
(u)
, where
p\in[1,\infty)
. If
1<p<\infty
, the class of locally convex spaces with the property
p
-
(u)
seems to be new even for Banach spaces.
It is well known that a Banach space
X
has the property
(u)
if and only if every
x^{\ast\ast}
in the weak
∗
sequential closure of
X
in its bidual is also the weak
∗
limit of a wuC series in
X
, see for example
[
63
, p. 32]
. In Proposition
12.7
we generalize this result to the property
p
-
(u)
in the class of all locally convex spaces. We show that the property
p
-
(u)
depends only on the duality
(E,E^{\prime})
and the class of locally convex spaces with the property
p
-
(u)
is stable under taking direct sums and Tychonoff products, see Propositions
12.8
and
12.9
.

One of the most important classes of operators between Banach spaces is the class of completely continuous operators. An operator
T
from a Banach space
X
into a Banach space
Y
is called
completely continuous
if it sends weakly convergent sequences into norm-convergent. This class of operators was generalized by Castillo and Sanchez
[
12
]
as follows: if
p\in[1,\infty]
,
T
is called
p
-convergent
if it sends weakly
p
-summable sequences in
X
into null-sequences in
Y
. The class of
p
-convergent operators plays a crucial role in characterization of Banach spaces with the Dunford–Pettis property of order
p
defined in
[
12
]
and the Gelfand–Phillips property of order
p
defined by Ghenciu
[
42
]
. It is natural to define an operator
T:E\to L
between locally convex spaces
E
and
L
to be
p
-convergent
if
T
sends weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
into null-sequences in
L
. In Theorem
13.17
of Section
13
, we characterize
p
-convergent operators
T:E\to L
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
. This theorem generalizes and extends the corresponding assertion for Banach spaces, see
[
12
, p. 45]
and Proposition 13 of
[
42
]
. Applying Theorem
13.17
to the identity operator
\mathsf{id}:E\to E
we obtain an operator characterization of locally convex spaces with the
p
-Schur property for the case
p\in[1,\infty)
, see Corollary
13.23
.

The results obtained in Section
13
are essentially used in Section
14
in which we
show natural relationships between images of bounded sets and
p
-convergence. Let
p\in[1,\infty]
, and let
T:E\to L
be an operator between locally convex spaces
E
and
L
. In Theorem
14.1
we prove that
T
sends bounded sets of
E
into
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of
L
if and only if the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
p
-convergent. As a consequence we obtain that every bounded subset of
E
is a
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
set if and only if
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
has the
p
-Schur property, see Corollary
14.2
. In Theorem
14.6
we show that if
E
is quasibarrelled, then
T
is
p
-convergent if and only if
T^{\ast}(B)
is a
(p,\infty)
-
(V)
set in
E^{\prime}
for every bounded subset
B
of
L^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Consequently, a quasibarrelled space
E
has the
p
-Schur property if and only if each bounded subset
B
of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(p,\infty)
-
(V)
set.
Generalizing and extending Theorem 15 of Ghenciu
[
42
]
we prove in Theorem
14.11
that the space
E
has the
wsV^{\ast}_{(p,\infty)}
property (resp., the
sV^{\ast}_{(p,\infty)}
property) if and only if for every normed space
Y
, if
T\in\mathcal{L}(Y,E)
is such that the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to Y^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
p
-convergent, then
T
is weakly sequentially precompact (resp., weakly sequentially compact). If additionally
E
is locally complete, then
E
has the
wsV^{\ast}_{(p,\infty)}
property (resp., the
sV^{\ast}_{(p,\infty)}
property) if and only if whenever
T\in\mathcal{L}(\ell_{1},E)
is such that the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to\ell_{\infty}
is
p
-convergent, then
T
is weakly sequentially precompact (resp., weakly sequentially compact).
Generalizing some results from
[
42
]
we give an operator characterization of
(p,\infty)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of
E
in the case when
1<p<\infty
(Theorem
14.16
), and an operator characterization of
(p,\infty)
-
(V)
subsets of
E^{\prime}
in the case when
1\leq p<\infty
(Theorem
14.23
).

In Section
15
we generalize some known results related to (weakly) compact operators between Banach spaces, see Proposition
15.1
and Theorem
15.3
. Being motivated by the classical Gantmacher theorem (see Theorem
15.5
below) we define an lcs
E
to have the
Gantmacher property
if for every Banach space
L
, an operator
T:E\to L
is weakly compact if and only if so is its adjoint
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Generalizing an operator characterization of the property
V
for Banach spaces obtained by Pełczyński in Proposition 1 of
[
71
]
, we show in Theorem
15.15
that an
\ell_{\infty}
-
V_{p}
-barrelled space
E
with the Gantmacher property has the property
sV_{p}
if and only if for every Banach space
L
, each
p
-convergent operator
T:E\to L
is weakly compact. Using this theorem and a strong result of Pełczyński
[
70
]
, we provide a short proof of Theorem
1.2
, see Theorem
15.16
.

In Section
16
we generalize the notion of
p
-convergent operators as follows. Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
. An operator
T
from an lcs
E
to a Banach space
L
is
(q,p)
-convergent
if it sends weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
to strongly
q
-summable sequences in
L
.
In Theorem
16.3
we show that
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent if and only if
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V)
subsets of
E^{\prime}
. If
T
is an operator from a normed space to
E
, then
T(B_{L})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of
E
if and only if
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
(q,p)
-convergent, see Theorem
16.8
. In Theorems
16.6
and
16.7
we characterize locally convex spaces for which every
(p,q)
-
(V)
subset of
E^{\prime}
is relatively weakly sequentially (
p
-)compact or relatively sequentially compact. An operator characterization of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of
E
is given in Theorem
16.11
. These results essentially generalize some of known results for Banach spaces, see for example Ghenciu
[
42
,
43
]
.

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
. An operator characterization of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of an lcs
E
given in Theorem
16.11
motivates the problem to find conditions under which an operator
T
from
E
to a Banach space
L
is
(q,p)
-convergent. In the case when
E
is also a Banach space such a conditions is known:
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent if and only if it is
(q,p)
-summing, see
[
24
, p. 197]
. In Section
17
we introduce the notion of
(q,p)
-summing operators between locally convex space (see Definition
17.2
) and show in Proposition
17.5
that every
(q,p)
-summing operator is
(q,p)
-convergent and the converse is true if the space
\ell_{p}[E]
is barrelled. We study the vector space of all
(q,p)
-summing operators and show that numerous basic results concerning
(q,p)
-summing operator between Banach spaces (as, for example, Ideal Property, Inclusion Property, Injectivity, Pietsch Domination Theorem and Pietsch Factorization Theorem) remain true in more general cases. As an application of the obtained results we show in Theorem
17.19
that
every Banach space
E
has the property
V^{\ast}_{(p,p)}
.

It should be mentioned that the obtained results will play a crucial role in the study of Dunford–Pettis type properties and Gelfand–Phillips type properties of locally convex spaces, see
[
38
]
and
[
39
]
.

2
Preliminary results

We start with some necessary definitions and notations used in the article. Set
\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
and
\omega:=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
.
All topological spaces are assumed to be Tychonoff (= completely regular and
T_{1}
). The closure of a subset
A
of a topological space
X
is denoted by
\overline{A}
,
\overline{A}^{X}
or
\mathrm{cl}_{X}(A)
.
Recall that a function
f:X\to Y
between topological spaces
X
and
Y
is called
sequentially continuous
if for any convergent sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\subseteq X
, the sequence
\{f(x_{n})\}_{n\in\omega}
converges in
Y
and
\lim_{n}f(x_{n})=f(\lim_{n}x_{n})
.

All topological vector spaces are over the field
\mathbb{F}
of real
\mathbb{R}
or complex
\mathbb{C}
numbers. The closed unit ball of the field
\mathbb{F}
is denoted by
\mathbb{D}
. We denote by
C(X)
the vector space of all continuous
\mathbb{F}
-valued functions on
X
. The space
C(X)
endowed with the pointwise topology or the compact-open topology is denoted by
C_{p}(X)
and
C_{k}(X)
, respectively.

Although the spaces of continuous functions are the most important classes of function spaces on a space
X
, there are other classes of (discontinuous) functions which are of significant importance and widely studied in General Topology and Analysis; for example, the classes of Baire type functions introduced and studied by Baire
[
2
]
.
Let
X
be a Tychonoff spaces. For
\alpha=0
, we put
B_{0}(X):=C_{p}(X)
. For every nonzero countable ordinal
\alpha
, let
B_{\alpha}(X)
be the family of all functions
f:X\to\mathbb{F}
that are pointwise limits of sequences
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}B_{\beta}(X)
and
B(X):=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}B_{\alpha}(X)
. All the spaces
B_{\alpha}(X)
and
B(X)
are endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence, inherited from the Tychonoff product
\mathbb{F}^{X}
.

Recall that a subset
A
of a topological space
X
is called

\bullet

relatively compact
if its closure
{\bar{A}}
is compact;

\bullet

(
relatively
)
countably compact
if each countably infinite subset in
A
has a cluster point in
A
(resp., of
X
);

\bullet

(
relatively
)
sequentially compact
if each sequence in
A
has a subsequence converging to a point of
A
(resp., of
X
);

\bullet

functionally bounded in
X
if every
f\in C(X)
is bounded on
A
.

The following lemma is well known and can be easily proved using the diagonal method (see also the proof of Lemma
2.2
below).

Lemma 2.1

Let
X:=\prod_{i\in\omega}X_{n}
be the Tychonoff product of a sequence
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
of topological spaces,
A
be a subset of
X
, and for every
n\in\omega
, let
A_{n}
be the projection of
A
onto
X_{n}
. Then
A
is a
(
relatively
)
sequentially compact subset of
X:=\prod_{i\in\omega}X_{n}
if and only if
A_{n}
is a
(
relatively
)
sequentially compact subset of
X_{n}
for all
n\in\omega
.

Recall that a Tychonoff space
X
is called
Fréchet–Urysohn
if for any cluster point
a\in X
of a subset
A\subseteq X
there is a sequence
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\subseteq A
which converges to
a
. A Tychonoff space
X
is called an
angelic space
if (1) every relatively countably compact subset of
X
is relatively compact, and (2) any compact subspace of
X
is Fréchet–Urysohn. Note that any subspace of an angelic space is angelic, and a subset
A
of an angelic space
X
is compact if and only if it is countably compact if and only if
A
is sequentially compact, see Lemma 0.3 of
[
76
]
. Note also that if
\tau
and
\nu
are regular topologies on a set
X
such that
\tau\leq\nu
and the space
(X,\tau)
is angelic, then the space
(X,\nu)
is also angelic, see
[
76
]
. By the famous Eberlein–Šmulyan theorem every Banach space with the weak topology is angelic. The Grothendieck extension of Eberlein–Šmulyan’s theorem states that
C_{p}(K)
is angelic for every compact space
K
.

Let
E
be a topological vector space (tvs for short). The span of a subset
A
of
E
and its closure are denoted by
\mathsf{span}(A)
and
\overline{\mathsf{span}}(A)
, respectively. We denote by
\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
(resp.,
\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
) the family of all (resp., closed absolutely convex) neighborhoods of zero of
E
. The family of all bounded subsets of
E
is denoted by
\mathsf{Bo}(E)
. The topological dual space of
E
is denoted by
E^{\prime}
. The value of
\chi\in E^{\prime}
on
x\in E
is denoted by
\langle\chi,x\rangle
or
\chi(x)
. If
E^{\prime}
separates the points of
E
,
E
is called
separated
. A sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
is said to be
Cauchy
if for every
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
there is
N\in\omega
such that
x_{n}-x_{m}\in U
for all
n,m\geq N
. It is easy to see that a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
is Cauchy if and only if
x_{n_{k}}-x_{n_{k+1}}\to 0
for every (strictly) increasing sequence
(n_{k})
in
\omega
. If
E
is a normed space,
B_{E}
denotes the closed unit ball of
E
.

Let
E
be a topological vector space. We denote by
E_{w}
and
E_{\beta}
the space
E
endowed with the weak topology
\sigma(E,E^{\prime})
or with the strong topology
\beta(E,E^{\prime})
, respectively. The topological dual space
E^{\prime}
of
E
endowed with weak
∗
topology
\sigma(E^{\prime},E)
or with the strong topology
\beta(E^{\prime},E)
is denoted by
E^{\prime}_{w^{\ast}}
or
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, respectively. The closure of a subset
A
in the weak topology is denoted by
\overline{A}^{\,w}
or
\overline{A}^{\,\sigma(E,E^{\prime})}
, and
\overline{B}^{\,w^{\ast}}
(or
\overline{B}^{\,\sigma(E^{\prime},E)}
) denotes the closure of
B\subseteq E^{\prime}
in the weak
∗
topology. The
polar
of a subset
A
of
E
is denoted by

A^{\circ}:=\{\chi\in E^{\prime}:\|\chi\|_{A}\leq 1\},\quad\mbox{ where }\quad\|\chi\|_{A}=\sup\big\{|\chi(x)|:x\in A\cup\{0\}\big\}.

A subset
B
of
E^{\prime}
is
equicontinuous
if
B\subseteq U^{\circ}
for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
.

Let
H
be a subspace of a separated tvs
E
. Then the
annihilator
H^{\perp}
of
H
is the subspace
E^{\prime}
defined by

H^{\perp}:=\{\chi\in E^{\prime}:\langle\chi,x\rangle=0\mbox{ for every }x\in H\}.

Two vector topologies
\tau
and
\mathcal{T}
on a vector space
L
are called
compatible
if
(L,\tau)^{\prime}=(L,\mathcal{T})^{\prime}
algebraically. If
(E,\tau)
is a locally convex space, then there is a finest locally convex vector topology
\mu(E,E^{\prime})
compatible with
\tau
. The topology
\mu(E,E^{\prime})
is called the
Mackey topology
, and if
\tau=\mu(E,E^{\prime})
, the space
E
is called a
Mackey space
. Set
E_{\mu}:=\big(E,\mu(E,E^{\prime})\big)
.

A subset
A
of a separated topological vector space
E
is called

\bullet

precompact
if for every
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
there is a finite set
F\subseteq E
such that
A\subseteq F+U
;

\bullet

sequentially precompact
if every sequence in
A
has a Cauchy subsequence.

It is easy to see that any precompact subset of
E
is bounded. It is well known that a subset of a locally convex space
E
is bounded if and only if it is weakly precompact. Using standard terminology we shall say that
A
is weakly compact (resp., weakly sequentially compact, relatively weakly compact, relatively weakly sequentially compact, or weakly sequentially precompact) if
A
is a compact (resp., sequentially compact, relatively compact, relatively sequentially compact, or sequentially precompact) subset of
E_{w}
.

Lemma 2.2

Any sequentially precompact subset
A
of a topological vector space
E
is precompact hence bounded, but the converse is not true in general.

Proof 1

Suppose for a contradiction that
A
is not precompact. Then there is
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
such that for every finite set
F\subseteq E
, it follows
A\not\subseteq F+U
. Take an arbitrary
a_{0}\in A{\setminus}U
. By induction on
n\geq 1
, choose
a_{n+1}\in A
such that
a_{n+1}\not\in\{a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\}+U
. Then, by construction,
a_{n}-a_{m}\not\in U
for every
n>m
. Therefore the sequence
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
does not contain a Cauchy subsequence and hence
A
is not sequentially precompact, a contradiction.

To show that the converse is not true in general we consider the following example. Let
E
be the Banach space
C(3^{\omega})
of all real-valued continuous functions on the product
3^{\omega}
endowed with the weak topology, where
3:=\{-1,0,1\}
is endowed with the discrete topology. For every
n\in\omega
, let
f_{n}
be the projection of the compact space
3^{\omega}
onto the
n
th coordinate. Then the sequence
S=\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
is equivalent to the standard unit basis of
\ell_{1}
.
Indeed, for every
n\in\omega
and each scalars
c_{0},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
, set

x:=\big(\mathrm{sign}(c_{0}),\dots,\mathrm{sign}(c_{n}),0,0,\dots\big)\in 3^{\omega}.

Then
\big|\sum_{i\leq n}c_{i}f_{i}(x)\big|=\sum_{i\leq n}|c_{i}|
, and hence
S
is an
\ell_{1}
-sequence in
C(3^{w})
. Therefore, by the Rosenthal
\ell_{1}
-theorem, the sequence
S
has no weakly Cauchy subsequences and hence it is not sequentially precompact in
E
. On the other hand, the set
S
, being a bounded subset of
C(3^{\omega})
, is precompact in the weak topology.∎

Let
E
and
L
be two topological vector spaces. The map
T
is
weak-weak
or
weakly continuous
if it is continuous as a map from
E_{w}
to
L_{w}
. If
T:E\to L
is a weakly continuous linear map, we denote by
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
the adjoint linear map defined by
\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle=\langle\chi,T(x)\rangle
for every
\chi\in L^{\prime}
and each
x\in E
. The family of all operators (= continuous linear maps) from
E
to
L
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}(E,L)
.
Observe that if
B\subseteq L^{\prime}
is equicontinuous and
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
, then
T^{\ast}(B)\subseteq E^{\prime}
is also equicontinuous. An operator
T:E\to L
is called
bounded-covering
if for every bounded subset
A
of
L
there is a bounded subset
B
of
E
such that
A\subseteq T(B)
. Evidently, bounded-covering operators are surjective. It is clear that if
H
is a closed subspace of a normed space
E
, then the quotient map
q:E\to E/H
is bounded-covering.

Let
E
and
L
be two locally convex spaces, and let
T:E\to L
be a surjective operator. We shall say that
T
is
almost bounded-covering
if for every bounded subset
A
of
L
there is a bounded subset
B
of
E
such that
A\subseteq\overline{T(B)}
. The meaning of almost bounded-covering maps is explained by the following assertion which is of independent interest.

Proposition 2.3

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
be surjective. Then the adjoint map
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is an embedding if and only if
T
is almost bounded-covering.

Proof 2

First we recall that
T^{\ast}
is an injective strongly continuous operator, see Theorem 8.11.3 of
[
67
]
. Denote by
H
the image of
T^{\ast}
in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
with the induced topology.

Now, assume that
T^{\ast}
is an embedding. So, for every bounded subset
A
of
L
, the image
T^{\ast}\big(A^{\circ}\big)
is a neighborhood of zero in
H
. This means that there is a closed absolutely convex bounded subset
B
of
E
such that
B^{\circ}\cap H\subseteq T^{\ast}\big(A^{\circ}\big)
. We show that
A\subseteq\overline{T(B)}
. Suppose for a contradiction that there is
z\in E
such that
T(z)\in A{\setminus}\overline{T(B)}
(we use here the surjectivity of
T
). Since
\overline{T(B)}
is bounded closed and absolutely convex, the strong separation theorem guaranties that there is
\eta\in L^{\prime}
such that
\eta\in\overline{T(B)}^{\,\circ}
but
\langle\eta,T(z)\rangle>1
. Then
T^{\ast}(\eta)\in B^{\circ}\cap H
but
\eta\not\in A^{\circ}
because
T(z)\in A
. Since
T^{\ast}
is injective it follows that
T^{\ast}(\eta)\not\in T^{\ast}\big(A^{\circ}\big)
and hence
B^{\circ}\cap H\nsubseteq T^{\ast}\big(A^{\circ}\big)
, a contradiction. Thus
A\subseteq\overline{T(B)}
and hence
T
is almost bounded-covering.

Conversely, assume that
T:E\to L
is almost bounded-covering. Since
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is injective and continuous it remains to show that
T^{\ast}
is open. Let
A^{\circ}
be a standard neighborhood of zero in
L^{\prime}_{\beta}
, where
A
is a bounded subset of
L
. We show that
T^{\ast}(A^{\circ})
is a neighborhood of zero in
H\subseteq E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. To this end, choose a bounded subset
B
of
E
such that
A\subseteq\overline{T(B)}
. Then every
\chi\in B^{\circ}\cap H
has the form
\chi=T^{\ast}(\eta)
for some
\eta\in L^{\prime}
such that

|\langle\eta,T(b)\rangle|=|\langle\chi,b\rangle|\leq 1\quad\mbox{ for every $b\in B$}.

Since
A\subseteq\overline{T(B)}
it follows that
\eta\in A^{\circ}
, and hence
\chi\in T^{\ast}(A^{\circ})
. Thus
T^{\ast}
is open.∎

Recall that an lcs
E
is called
semi-reflexive
if the canonical map
J_{E}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime}_{\beta})^{\prime}_{\beta}
defined by
\langle J_{E}(x),\chi\rangle:=\langle\chi,x\rangle
(
\chi\in E^{\prime}
) is an isomorphism; if in addition
J_{E}
is a topological isomorphism the space
E
is called
reflexive
.

Let
H
be a dense subspace of a locally convex space
E
, and let
\mathrm{id}:H\to E
be the identity embedding. Then the adjoint map
\mathrm{id}^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to H^{\prime}_{\beta}
is continuous. Since
E^{\prime}=H^{\prime}
algebraically, this means that the strong topology of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is finer than the strong topology of
H^{\prime}_{\beta}
. It is natural to ask when both topologies coincide, in other words, when
E^{\prime}_{\beta}=H^{\prime}_{\beta}
? This is done in the following assertion.
We recall that a dense subspace
H
of a locally convex space
E
is called
large
if every bounded subset of
E
is contained in the closure of a bounded subset of
H
(for more information on large subspaces of
E
, see Chapter 8.3 of
[
72
]
).

Proposition 2.4

Let
H
be a dense subspace of a locally convex space
E
. Then
E^{\prime}_{\beta}=H^{\prime}_{\beta}
if and only if
H
is large.

Proof 3

Assume that
E^{\prime}_{\beta}=H^{\prime}_{\beta}
, and let
A
be a closed absolutely convex bounded subset of
E
. Since
E^{\prime}_{\beta}=H^{\prime}_{\beta}
, there is a closed absolutely convex bounded subset
B
of
H
such that
B^{\circ}\subseteq A^{\circ}
. We show that
A\subseteq\overline{B}^{\,E}
. First we note that
A=A^{\circ\circ}\subseteq B^{\circ\circ}
, where the second polar of
B
is taken in the space
E
. Observe that
B
is absolutely convex in
E
as well, and hence
\overline{B}^{\,E}
is also absolutely convex. Therefore, by the Bipolar Theorem,
B^{\circ\circ}=\big(\overline{B}^{\,E}\big)^{\circ\circ}=\overline{B}^{\,E}
. Thus
A\subseteq\overline{B}^{\,E}
, and hence
H
is large in
E
.

Conversely, assume that
H
is large in
E
, and let
A^{\circ}
be a standard neighborhood of zero in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, where
A\subseteq E
is bounded. Choose a bounded subset
B
of
H
such that
A\subseteq\overline{B}^{\,E}
. Then
B^{\circ}\subseteq A^{\circ}
and hence the topology of
H^{\prime}_{\beta}
is finer than the topology of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Since the topology of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is always finer than the topology of
H^{\prime}_{\beta}
we obtain
E^{\prime}_{\beta}=H^{\prime}_{\beta}
.∎

We denote by
\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i}
and
\prod_{i\in I}E_{i}
the locally convex direct sum and the topological product of a non-empty family
\{E_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of locally convex spaces, respectively. If
\mathbf{x}=(x_{i})\in\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i}
, then the set
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{x}):=\{i\in I:x_{i}\not=0\}
is called the
support
of
\mathbf{x}
. The
support
of a subset
A
of
\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i}
is the set
\mathrm{supp}(A):=\bigcup_{a\in A}\mathrm{supp}(a)
. We shall also consider elements
\mathbf{x}=(x_{i})\in\prod_{i\in I}E_{i}
as functions on
I
and write
\mathbf{x}(i):=x_{i}
.
The following assertion is proved in
[
57
, p. 287]
.

Proposition 2.5

Let
\{E_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a non-empty family of locally convex spaces. Then:

\Big(\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i}\Big)^{\prime}_{\beta}=\prod_{i\in I}\big(E_{i}\big)^{\prime}_{\beta}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\Big(\prod_{i\in I}E_{i}\Big)^{\prime}_{\beta}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\big(E_{i}\big)^{\prime}_{\beta}.

The next easy result will be used in what follows.

Lemma 2.6

Let
E=\prod_{i\in I}E_{i}
be the direct product of a non-empty family
\{E_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of locally convex spaces, and let
L
be a normed space. Then for every
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
, there is a finite subset
F
of
I
such that
\{0\}^{F}\times\prod_{i\in I{\setminus}F}E_{i}
is contained in the kernel of
T
.

Proof 4

Take a finite subset
F
of
I
such that
T\big(\{0\}^{F}\times\prod_{i\in I{\setminus}F}E_{i}\big)
is contained in the unit ball
B_{L}
. Since
B_{L}
contains no non-trivial vector subspaces it follows that
T\big(\{0\}^{F}\times\prod_{i\in I{\setminus}F}E_{i}\big)=\{0\}
, as desired. ∎

We shall use also the next lemma which complements Lemma
2.1
.

Lemma 2.7

Let
E:=\prod_{i\in\omega}E_{n}
be the product of a sequence
\{E_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
of locally convex spaces,
A
be a subset of
E
, and let
A_{n}
be the projection of
A
onto
E_{n}
for every
n\in\omega
. Then
A
is a
(
weakly
)
sequentially precompact subset of
E
if and only if
A_{n}
is a
(
weakly
)
sequentially precompact subset of
E_{n}
for all
n\in\omega
.

Proof 5

By Theorem 8.8.5 of
[
52
]
, we have
E_{w}=\prod_{i\in\omega}(E_{n})_{w}
. Therefore it suffices to prove the lemma only for the sequentially precompact case. The necessity is clear. To prove the sufficiency, let
\{\mathbf{a}_{k}\}_{k\in\omega}
be a sequence in
A
. Since
A_{0}
is sequentially precompact in
E_{0}
, there is a sequence
J_{0}\subseteq\omega
such that the sequence
\{\mathbf{a}_{k}(0)\}_{k\in J_{0}}
is Cauchy in
E_{0}
. Since
A_{1}
is sequentially precompact in
E_{1}
, there is a sequence
J_{1}\subseteq J_{0}
such that
\{\mathbf{a}_{k}(1)\}_{k\in J_{1}}
is Cauchy in
E_{1}
. By induction we can find a sequence
J_{i+1}\subseteq J_{i}
such that
\{\mathbf{a}_{k}(i+1)\}_{k\in J_{i+1}}
is Cauchy in
E_{i+1}
. For every
s\in\omega
, choose
k_{s}\in J_{s}
such that the sequence
(k_{s})\subseteq\omega
is strictly increasing. It is easy to see that the subsequence
\{\mathbf{a}_{k_{s}}\}_{s\in\omega}
of
\{\mathbf{a}_{k}\}_{k\in\omega}
is Cauchy in the product
E
. Thus
A
is sequentially precompact.∎

We denote by
\mathsf{ind}_{n\in\omega}E_{n}
the inductive limit of a (reduced) inductive sequence
\big\{(E_{n},\tau_{n})\big\}_{n\in\omega}
of locally convex spaces. If in addition
\tau_{m}{\restriction}_{E_{n}}=\tau_{n}
for all
n,m\in\omega
with
n\leq m
, the inductive limit
\mathsf{ind}_{n\in\omega}E_{n}
is called
strict
and is denoted by
\underrightarrow{\mbox{ s-$\mathsf{ind}$}}_{n}\,E_{n}
. It is well known that
E:=\underrightarrow{\mbox{ s-$\mathsf{ind}$}}_{n}\,E_{n}
is regular, i.e., every bounded set in
E
is contained in some
E_{n}
. For more details we refer the reader to Section 4.5 of
[
52
]
. In the partial case when all spaces
E_{n}
are Fréchet, the strict inductive limit is called a
strict
(LF)
-space
. One of the most important examples of strict
(LF)
-spaces is the space
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
of test functions over an open subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The strong dual
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
of
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
is the space of distributions.

Lemma 2.8

Let
E
be a locally convex space whose closed bounded sets are weakly angelic
(
for example,
E
is a strict
(LF)
-space
)
. Then
E
is weakly angelic.

Proof 6

Let
A
be a relatively weakly countably compact subset of
E
. By assumption,
\overline{A}^{\,w}
is an angelic space. Therefore
\overline{A}^{\,w}
is compact and Fréchet–Urysohn. In particular,
A
is relatively weakly compact. Thus
E
is a weakly angelic space.

Assume that
E=\underrightarrow{\mbox{ s-$\mathsf{ind}$}}_{n}\,E_{n}
is a strict
(LF)
-space. Then any bounded set of
E
is sit in some
E_{n}
and
E_{n}
is (weakly) closed in
E
. Therefore it suffices to show that any Fréchet space is weakly angelic.

It is well known that any Fréchet space
E
embeds into the product
\prod_{n\in\omega}X_{n}
of a sequence
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
of Banach spaces, see Proposition 8.5.4 of
[
52
]
, where
X_{n}=C(K_{n})
for some compact space
K_{n}
. By Proposition 4 of
[
47
]
,
X_{n}
is even a strictly angelic space. Thus, by Theorem 1 of
[
47
]
,
\prod_{n\in\omega}X_{n}
and hence also
E
are strictly angelic in the weak topology.∎

It is well known that any two compatible locally convex vector topologies on a vector space have the same bounded sets. Let us recall that a locally convex space
(E,\tau)
has

\bullet

the
Schur property
if
E
and
E_{w}
have the same convergent sequences;

\bullet

the
Glicksberg property
if
E
and
E_{w}
have the same compact sets.

If an lcs
E
has the Glicksberg property, then it has trivially the Schur property. The converse is true for strict
(LF)
-spaces (in particular, for Banach spaces), but not in general, see Corollary 2.13 and Proposition 3.5 of
[
35
]
.

Below we recall the basic classical weak barrelledness conditions, for more details see the books
[
52
]
,
[
57
]
,
[
67
]
, or
[
72
]
.

Definition 2.9

A locally convex space
E
is called

\bullet

(quasi)barrelled if every
\sigma(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded (resp.,
\beta(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded) subset of
E^{\prime}
is equicontinuous;

\bullet

\aleph_{0}
-(quasi)barrelled if every
\sigma(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded (resp.,
\beta(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded) subset of
E^{\prime}
which is the countable union of equicontinuous subsets is also equicontinuous;

\bullet

\ell_{\infty}
-(quasi)barrelled if every
\sigma(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded (resp.,
\beta(E^{\prime},E)
-bounded) sequence is equicontinuous;

\bullet

c_{0}
-(quasi)barrelled if every
\sigma(E^{\prime},E)
-null (resp.,
\beta(E^{\prime},E)
-null) sequence is equicontinuous.∎

Below we recall several important classes of locally convex spaces which play an essential role in the article, in particular, to construct (counter)examples.

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
. Then the conjugate number
p^{\ast}
of
p
is defined to be the unique element of
[1,\infty]
which satisfies
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{p^{\ast}}=1
. For
p\in[1,\infty)
, the space
\ell_{p^{\ast}}
is the dual space of
\ell_{p}
. We denote by
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
the canonical basis of
\ell_{p}
, if
1\leq p<\infty
, or the canonical basis of
c_{0}
, if
p=\infty
. The canonical basis of
\ell_{p^{\ast}}
is denoted by
\{e_{n}^{\ast}\}_{n\in\omega}
. In what follows we always identify
\ell_{1^{\ast}}
with
c_{0}
. Denote by
\ell_{p}^{0}
and
c_{0}^{0}
the linear span of
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
\ell_{p}
or
c_{0}
endowed with the induced norm topology, respectively. We shall use also the following well known description of relatively compact subsets of
\ell_{p}
and
c_{0}
, see
[
20
, p. 6]
.

Proposition 2.10

(i)
A bounded subset
A
of
\ell_{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, is relatively compact if and only if

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup\Big\{\sum_{m\leq n}|x_{n}|^{p}:x=(x_{n})\in A\Big\}=0.

(ii)
A bounded subset
A
of
c_{0}
is relatively compact if and only if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup\{|x_{n}|:x=(x_{n})\in A\}=0.

We denote by
E=(c_{0})_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{\omega}
the Banach space
c_{0}
endowed with the pointwise topology inherited from the direct product
\mathbb{F}^{\omega}
.
The direct locally convex sum
\mathbb{F}^{(\omega)}
of a countably-infinite family of the field
\mathbb{F}
is denoted by
\varphi
. It is well known that any bounded subset of
\varphi
is finite-dimensional, see
[
52
]
. We denote by
\{e_{n}^{\ast}\}_{n\in\omega}
the standard coordinate basis of
\varphi
. Observe that
(\mathbb{F}^{\omega})^{\prime}_{\beta}=\varphi
.

One of the most important classes of locally convex spaces is the class of free locally convex spaces introduced by Markov in
[
65
]
. The
free locally convex space

L(X)
over a Tychonoff space
X
is a pair consisting of a locally convex space
L(X)
and a continuous map
i:X\to L(X)
such that every continuous map
f
from
X
to a locally convex space
E
gives rise to a unique continuous linear operator
\Psi_{E}(f):L(X)\to E
with
f=\Psi_{E}(f)\circ i
. The free locally convex space
L(X)
always exists and is essentially unique, and
X
is the Hamel basis of
L(X)
. So, each nonzero
\chi\in L(X)
has a unique decomposition
\chi=a_{1}i(x_{1})+\cdots+a_{n}i(x_{n})
, where all
a_{k}
are nonzero and
x_{k}
are distinct. The set
\mathrm{supp}(\chi):=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
is called the
support
of
\chi
. In what follows we shall identify
i(x)
with
x
and consider
i(x)
as the Dirac measure
\delta_{x}
at the point
x\in X
. We also recall that
C_{p}(X)^{\prime}=L(X)
and
L(X)^{\prime}=C(X)
. It is worth mentioning that
L(X)
has the Glicksberg property for every Tychonoff space
X
, and if
X
is non-discrete, then
L(X)
is not a Mackey space, see
[
36
]
and
[
33
]
, respectively.

3
Completeness type properties in locally convex spaces

Let
E
be a topological vector space. We shall say that a subfamily
\mathcal{A}(E)
of
\mathsf{Bo}(E)
is a
bornology on
E
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

if
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}(E)
, then there is
A\in\mathcal{A}(E)
such that
(A_{1}+A_{2})\cup A_{1}\cup A_{2}\subseteq A
;

(b)

if
A\in\mathcal{A}(E)
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
, then
\lambda A\in\mathcal{A}(E)
;

(c)

\mathcal{A}(E)
contains all finite subsets of
E
and is closed under taking subsets.

Any bornology
\mathcal{A}(E)
on
E
defines the
\mathcal{A}
-topology
denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
on the dual space
E^{\prime}
of uniform convergence on the members of
\mathcal{A}
. We write
E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}:=(E^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})
. Recall that the
saturated hull
\mathcal{A}^{sat}
of
\mathcal{A}
is the collection of all subsets of bipolars of elements of
\mathcal{A}
, and the bornology
\mathcal{A}
is
saturated
if
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{sat}
.

Let us recall some of the most important families of compact-type subsets of a separated topological vector space
E
which form a bornology on
E
. These families complete the family
\mathcal{PC}(E)
of precompact sets and the family
\mathcal{SPC}(E)
of sequentially precompact sets considered in Section
2
. A subset
A
of
E
is called

\bullet

weakly
(
sequentially
)
compact
if
A
is (sequentially) compact in
E_{w}
;

\bullet

relatively weakly compact
if its weak closure
\overline{A}^{\,\sigma(E,E^{\prime})}
is compact in
E_{w}
;

\bullet

relatively weakly sequentially compact
if each sequence in
A
has a subsequence weakly converging to a point of
E
;

\bullet

weakly sequentially precompact
if each sequence in
A
has a weakly Cauchy subsequence.

Notation 3.1

For a separated tvs
E
, we denote by
\mathcal{A}(E)
one of the families
\mathcal{RC}(E)
,
\mathcal{RSC}(E)
,
\mathcal{PC}(E)
,
\mathcal{SPC}(E)
,
\mathcal{RWC}(E)
,
\mathcal{RWSC}(E)
,
\mathcal{WSPC}(E)
, or
\mathsf{Bo}(E)
of all relatively compact, relatively sequentially compact, precompact, sequentially precompact, relatively weakly compact, relatively weakly sequentially compact, weakly sequentially precompact or bounded subsets of
E
, respectively.∎

It is easy to see that all the families in Notation
3.1
are bornologies.

Below we recall some of the basic classes of compact-type operators.

Definition 3.2

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces. An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
is called compact (resp., sequentially compact, precompact, sequentially precompact, weakly compact, weakly sequentially compact, weakly sequentially precompact, bounded) if there is
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
such that
T(U)\in\mathcal{RC}(L)
(resp.,
\mathcal{RSC}(L)
,
\mathcal{PC}(L)
,
\mathcal{SPC}(L)
,
\mathcal{RWC}(L)
,
\mathcal{RWSC}(L)
,
\mathcal{WSPC}(L)
, or
\mathsf{Bo}(L)
). The family of all such operators is denoted by
\mathcal{LC}(E,L)
(resp.,
\mathcal{LSC}(E,L)
,
\mathcal{LPC}(E,L)
,
\mathcal{LSPC}(E,L)
,
\mathcal{LWC}(E,L)
,
\mathcal{LWSC}(E,L)
,
\mathcal{LWSPC}(E,L)
, or
\mathcal{LB}(E,L)
). ∎

It is natural to generalize and unify Definition
3.2
as follows.

Definition 3.3

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
\mathcal{A}(L)
be a bornology on
L
. An operator
T:E\to L
belongs to
\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
if there is
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
such that
T(U)\in\mathcal{A}(L)
.∎

To obtain some characterizations of compact type operators it is essential to find additional conditions on
E
under which the family
\mathcal{A}(E)
is saturated.
This can be naturally done using completeness type properties of
E
. Let us recall that a locally convex space
E

\bullet

is
complete
if each Cauchy net converges;

\bullet

is
quasi-complete
if each closed bounded subset of
E
is complete;

\bullet

is
von Neumann complete
if every precompact subset of
E
is relatively compact;

\bullet

is
sequentially complete
if each Cauchy sequence in
E
converges;

\bullet

is
locally complete
if the closed absolutely convex hull of a null sequence in
E
is compact;

\bullet

has the
convex compactness property
(ccp) if the closed absolutely convex hull of each compact subset
K
of
E
is compact;

\bullet

has the
Krein property
if the closed absolutely convex hull of each weakly compact subset
K
of
E
is weakly compact (i.e.,
E_{w}
has the ccp).

The Krein property was defined and characterized in
[
35
]
being motivated by the Krein theorem
[
57
, § 24.5(4)]
which states that if
K
is a weakly compact subset of an lcs
E
, then
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(K)
is weakly compact if and only if
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(K)
is
\mu(E,E^{\prime})
-complete. Therefore
E
is a Krein space if the space
E_{\mu}:=(E,\mu(E,E^{\prime}))
is quasi-complete. In the next lemma the clause (i) is well known and the clauses (ii) and (iii) follow from the corresponding definitions.

Lemma 3.4

Let
E
be a locally convex space, and let
\mathcal{A}(E)
be a bornology on
E
. Then
\mathcal{A}(E)
is saturated if one of the following condition holds:

(i)

\mathcal{A}(E)=\mathcal{PC}(E)
or
\mathcal{A}(E)=\mathsf{Bo}(E)
;

(ii)

E
has ccp and
\mathcal{A}(E)=\mathcal{RC}(E)
;

(iii)

E
has the Krein property and
\mathcal{A}(E)=\mathcal{RWC}(E)
.

Lemma 3.5

A subset
A
of a sequentially complete separated tvs
E
is sequentially precompact if and only if it is relatively sequentially compact in
E
.

Proof 7

The sufficiency is clear. To prove the necessity, let
A
be a sequentially precompact subset of
E
. Take an arbitrary sequence
\{a_{n}\big\}_{n\in\omega}
in
A
, and choose a Cauchy subsequence
\big\{a_{n_{k}}\big\}_{k\in\omega}
in
\big\{a_{n}\big\}_{n\in\omega}
. Since
E
is sequentially complete, there is
x\in E
such that
a_{n_{k}}\to x
. Thus
A
is relatively sequentially compact in
E
.∎

Let
A
be a
disk
(= a bounded and absolutely convex subset) of a locally convex space
E
. We denote by
E_{A}
the linear span of
A
. The gauge
\rho_{A}
of
A
defines the norm topology on
E_{A}
. We shall use repeatedly the following result, see Proposition 3.2.2 and Proposition 5.1.6 of
[
72
]
.

Proposition 3.6

Let
A
be a disk in a locally convex space
E
. Then
(E_{A},\rho_{A})
is a normed space and its norm topology is finer than the topology induced from
E
. Moreover, if
E
is locally complete and
A
is closed, then
(E_{A},\rho_{A})
is a Banach space.

We shall use the following extension result. Recall that a subspace
Y
of a topological space
X
is
sequentially dense
if for every
x\in X
there is a sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
Y
which converges to
x
.

Proposition 3.7

Let
H
be a sequentially dense subspace of a locally convex space
E
,
L
be a sequentially complete locally convex space, and let
T:H\to L
be a bounded operator. Then
T
extends to a bounded operator
\overline{T}
from
E
to
L
. Consequently, any bounded operator from a metrizable locally convex space
E
to a sequentially complete locally convex space
L
can be extended to the completion
\overline{E}
of
E
.

Proof 8

For every
x\in E
, choose a sequence
S=\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
H
which converges to
x
. Then
S
is Cauchy in
H
, and hence the sequence
T(S)
is Cauchy in
L
. Since
L
is sequentially complete,
T(S)
converges to some element
l
. It is easy to see that
l
does not depend on a sequence converging to
x
, therefore we can define
\overline{T}(x):=l
. Evidently,
\overline{T}
is a linear map. It remains to prove that
\overline{T}
is bounded. To this end, fix an absolutely convex, closed neighborhood
U
of zero in
E
such that
T(U\cap H)
is a bounded subset of
L
. Set
W:=\tfrac{1}{2}U
. We show that
\overline{T}(W)
is a bounded subset of
L
. Let
x\in W
. Choose a sequence
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
H
which converges to
x
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\subseteq U\cap H
. Therefore
\overline{T}(x)
belongs to the bounded subset
\overline{T(U\cap H)}
of
L
. Thus
\overline{T}(W)
is bounded in
L
, as desired.∎

One of the basic theorems in Analysis is the Ascoli theorem which states that
if
X
is a
k
-space, then every compact subset of
C_{k}(X)
is evenly continuous
, see Theorem 3.4.20 in
[
28
]
. The Ascoli theorem motivates us in
[
3
]
to introduce and study the class of Ascoli spaces. A space
X
is called
Ascoli
if every compact subset of
C_{k}(X)
is evenly continuous. One can easily show that a compact subset of
C_{k}(X)
is evenly continuous if and only if it is equicontinuous. Recall that a subset
H
of
C(X)
is
equicontinuous
if for every
x\in X
and each
\varepsilon>0
there is an open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
|f(x^{\prime})-f(x)|<\varepsilon
for all
x^{\prime}\in U
and
f\in H
. Being motivated by the classical notion of
c_{0}
-barrelled locally convex spaces we defined in
[
34
]
a space
X
to be
sequentially Ascoli
if every convergent sequence in
C_{k}(X)
is equicontinuous. Clearly, every Ascoli space is sequentially Ascoli, but the converse is not true in general (every non-discrete
P
-space is sequentially Ascoli but not Ascoli, see Proposition 2.9 in
[
34
]
). In
[
37
]
, we proved that the space
C_{k}(X)
is locally complete if and only if
X
is a sequentially Ascoli space. The next assertion complements this result.

Proposition 3.8

If
X
is an Ascoli space, then
C_{k}(X)
has ccp.

Proof 9

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a compact subset of
C_{k}(X)
. Since
X
is Ascoli,
\mathcal{K}
is equicontinuous. Therefore, by Lemma 3.4.17 of
[
28
]
,
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(\mathcal{K})
is equcontinuous as well. As
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(\mathcal{K})
is also pointwise bounded and closed, the necessity of the Ascoli theorem
[
28
, 3.4.20]
implies that
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(\mathcal{K})
is a compact subset of
C_{k}(X)
. Thus
C_{k}(X)
has ccp.∎

We shall consider the following completeness type properties.

Definition 3.9

A topological vector space
E
is said to be

\bullet

separably quasi-complete if every closed separable bounded subset of
E
is complete;

\bullet

separably von Neumann complete if every closed separable precompact subset of
E
is compact.

Note that in the realm of topological vector spaces, separably von Neumann complete spaces coincide with topological vector spaces with the
\mathbf{cp}
-property introduced in
[
36
]
.

Proposition 3.10

Let
E
be a topological vector space. Then:

(i)

if
E
is quasi-complete, then it is separably quasi-complete and von Neumann complete;

(ii)

if
E
is either separably quasi-complete or von Neumann complete, then it is separably von Neumann complete;

(iii)

if
E
is separably von Neumann complete, then it is sequentially complete.

Proof 10

The assertions (i) and (ii) are evident (recall that any precompact set is bounded). To prove (iii) we note that every Cauchy sequence
S=\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
is a precompact separable set and hence its closure is compact. Then
S
has a cluster point
z\in E
. Since
S
is Cauchy it follows that
S
converges to the point
z
. Thus
E
is sequentially complete.∎

Example 3.11

The space
E=(\ell_{1})_{w}
is sequentially complete but not separably von Neumann complete.

Proof 11

The space
E
is sequentially complete by Corollary 10.5.4 of
[
52
]
(or by (i) of Proposition
5.2
below). Observe that
B_{\ell_{1}}
is closed, separable and precompact (being bounded) in the weak topology. However, by the Schur property,
B_{\ell_{1}}
is not compact in
E
. Thus
E
is not separably von Neumann complete.∎

Example 3.12

Let
\lambda
be an uncountable cardinal, and let

E:=\big\{(x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{\lambda}:|\mathrm{supp}(x_{i})|\leq\aleph_{0}\big\}.

Then
E
is a separably quasi-complete but not von Neumann complete space.

Proof 12

To show that
E
is separably quasi-complete, let
A
be a separable bounded subset of
E
. Take a dense countable subset
A_{0}
of
A
. Then
A_{0}
has countable support
I\subseteq\lambda
. Therefore the closure
\overline{A}=\overline{A_{0}}
in
E
has the same countable support
I
, and hence
\overline{A}
is a closed, bounded subset of the complete metrizable subspace
\mathbb{R}^{I}
of
E
. Whence
\overline{A}
is compact in
\mathbb{R}^{I}
and hence in
E
. Thus
E
is separably quasi-complete.

To show that
E
is not von Neumann complete, consider the following set

B:=\big\{(x_{i})\in E:x_{i}=1\mbox{ for every }i\in\mathrm{supp}(x_{i})\big\}.

Since the closure
\mathrm{cl}_{\mathbb{R}^{\lambda}}(B)
of
B
in the complete space
\mathbb{R}^{\lambda}
is compact and contains
(1_{i})\not\in E
, it follows that
B
is precompact but its closure in
E
is not compact. Thus
E
is not von Neumann complete.∎

The aforementioned results show the following relationships between the introduced completeness type properties (where “vN” means “von Neumann”)

complete
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{quasi-}\\
\mbox{complete}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{separably }\\
\mbox{quasi-complete}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{separably }\\
\mbox{vN complete}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{sequentially}\\
\mbox{complete}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{locally}\\
\mbox{complete}\end{subarray}}.}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{vN}\\
\mbox{complete}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{?}
ccp

Let
X
be a Tychonoff space. It is known that
C_{p}(X)
is quasi-complete if and only if
X
is discrete, see Theorem 3.6.6 of
[
52
]
. Below we generalize this result.

Theorem 3.13

Let
X
be a Tychonoff space. Then
C_{p}(X)
is von Neumann complete if and only if
X
is discrete.

Proof 13

If
X
is discrete, then
C_{p}(X)=\mathbb{F}^{X}
is even complete. Assume that
C_{p}(X)
is von Neumann complete. It is easy to see that the set
B:=\{f\in C(X):\|f\|_{X}\leq 1\}
is a closed precompact subset of
C_{p}(X)
. Therefore
B
is compact. Since
B
is dense in the compact space
\mathbb{D}^{X}
it follows that
B=\mathbb{D}^{X}
. So every bounded function on
X
is continuous. Thus
X
is discrete.∎

Recall that a Tychonoff space is called a
P
-space
if any countable intersection of open sets is open. The Buchwalter–Schmets theorem states that
C_{p}(X)
is sequentially complete if and only if
X
is a
P
-space, and, by Theorem 1.1 of
[
30
]
, the space
C_{p}(X)
is locally complete if and only if
X
is a
P
-space.
In Theorem
3.15
below we strengthen these results and give an independent and simpler proof of the implication (iv)
\Rightarrow
(i) using the following functional characterization of (non)
P
-spaces. Recall that the
support
of a continuous function
f\in C(X)
is the set
\mathrm{supp}(f):=\mathrm{cl}_{X}\{x\in X:f(x)\not=0\}
.

Proposition 3.14

For a Tychonoff space
X
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

X
is not a
P
-space;

(ii)

there is
f\in C(X)
such that the set
\{x\in X:f(x)>0\}
is not closed;

(iii)

there are a point
z\in X
, a sequence
\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
of continuous functions from
X
to
[0,2]
, and a sequence
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
of open subsets of
X
such that

(a)

\mathrm{supp}(g_{i})\subseteq U_{i}
for every
i\in\omega
;

(b)

U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\emptyset
for all distinct
i,j\in\omega
;

(c)

z\not\in U_{i}
for every
i\in\kappa
and
z\in\mathrm{cl}\big(\bigcup_{i\in\omega}\{x\in X:g_{i}(x)\geq 1\}\big)
.

Proof 14

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Suppose for a contradiction that the set
C_{f}:=\{x\in X:f(x)>0\}
is closed for every
f\in C(X)
. To get a contradiction we show that
X
is a
P
-space. To this end, let
\{U_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a decreasing sequence of open sets such that
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n}
is not empty. Assuming that
\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n}
is not open we could find a point
z\in\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n}{\setminus}\mathsf{Int}\big(\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n}\big)
. For every
n\in\omega
, choose a continuous function
h_{n}:X\to[0,1]
such that
h_{n}(X{\setminus}U_{n})=\{0\}
and
h_{n}(z)=1
. Then the function

h:=\sum_{n\in\omega}\tfrac{1}{2^{n+1}}\,h_{n}:X\to[0,1]

is continuous and such that
h(z)=1
. Set
f:=1-h
. Then
f:X\to[0,1]
is continuous and
f(z)=0
. By assumption the set
C_{f}
is closed and hence the set
X{\setminus}C_{f}
is open and contains
z
. Since

X{\setminus}C_{f}=\{x\in X:h(x)=1\}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\omega}\{x\in X:h_{n}(x)=1\}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n},

we obtain that
z\in\mathsf{Int}\big(\bigcap_{n\in\omega}U_{n}\big)
. But this contradicts the choice of
z
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) is proved in Case 1 of Proposition 2.4 of
[
33
]
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) For every
i\in\omega
, set
V_{i}:=\{x\in X:g_{i}(x)<1\}
. Since
\mathrm{supp}(g_{i})\subseteq U_{i}
and
z\not\in U_{i}
, it follows that
g_{i}(z)=0
and hence
V_{i}
is an open neighborhood of
z
. Applying (c) we obtain that
\bigcap_{i\in\omega}V_{i}
is not a neighborhood of
z
, and hence
\bigcap_{i\in\omega}V_{i}
is not open. Thus
X
is not a
P
-space.∎

Theorem 3.15

Let
X
be a Tychonoff space. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

X
is a
P
-space;

(ii)

C_{p}(X)
is separably quasi-complete;

(iii)

C_{p}(X)
is sequentially complete;

(iv)

C_{p}(X)
is locally complete.

Proof 15

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Assume that
X
is a
P
-space. Fix a separable closed bounded subset
B
of
C_{p}(X)
, and let
S=\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a dense subset of
B
. To show that
B
is complete, it suffices to show that
B
is closed in the completion
\mathbb{F}^{X}
of
C_{p}(X)
. To this end, it is sufficient to show that any
g\in\mathrm{cl}_{\mathbb{F}^{X}}(B)
is continuous (indeed, if
g\in C_{p}(X)
, then the closeness of
B
in
C_{p}(X)
implies that
g\in B
). Fix an arbitrary point
z\in X
. Since
X
is a
P
-space, there is a neighborhood
U
of
z
such that all functions
f_{n}
are constant on
U
. Since
S
is dense in
B
we have
\mathrm{cl}_{\mathbb{F}^{X}}(S)=\mathrm{cl}_{\mathbb{F}^{X}}(B)
, and hence the function
g
is also a constant function on
U
. Thus
g
is continuous at
z
, as desired.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) follows from Proposition
3.10
, and the implication (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) is well known (see Corollary 5.1.8
[
72
]
).

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i) Suppose for a contradiction that
X
is not a
P
-space. Then, by Proposition
3.14
, there are a point
z\in X
, a sequence
\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
of continuous functions from
X
to
[0,2]
, and a sequence
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
of open subsets of
X
such that

(a)

\mathrm{supp}(g_{i})\subseteq U_{i}
for every
i\in\omega
;

(b)

U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\emptyset
for all distinct
i,j\in\omega
;

(c)

z\not\in U_{i}
for every
i\in\kappa
and
z\in\mathrm{cl}\big(\bigcup_{i\in\omega}\{x\in X:g_{i}(x)\geq 1\}\big)
.

For every
n\in\omega
, set
f_{n}:=2^{n+1}g_{n}
. Then (a) and (b) imply that
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
is a null sequence in
C_{p}(X)
. For every
n\in\omega
, set

F_{n}(x):=\sum_{i\leq n}\tfrac{1}{2^{i+1}}\,f_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\leq n}g_{i}(x)\in\overline{\mathrm{acx}}\big(\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\big).

Since
C_{p}(X)
is locally complete, it follows that
\overline{\mathrm{acx}}\big(\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\big)
is compact and hence the sequence
\{F_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
has a cluster point
F\in C_{p}(X)
. By (c),
F_{n}(z)=0
for every
n\in\omega
, and hence
F(z)=0
. Choose a neighborhood
U
of
z
such that
F(x)<\tfrac{1}{4}
for every
x\in U
. By (c), there is an
m\in\omega
and a point
y\in U
such that
g_{m}(y)\geq 1
. Therefore, for every
n\geq m
, we have
F_{n}(y)\geq g_{m}(y)\geq 1
and hence

F(y)\geq\inf\{F_{n}(y):n\geq m\}\geq 1.

However, since
y\in U
we have
F(y)<\tfrac{1}{4}
. This is a desired contradiction. ∎

We finish this section with the following natural problems.

Problem 3.16

Characterize Tychonoff spaces
X
for which the space
C_{p}(X)
has ccp.

Problem 3.17

Characterize Tychonoff spaces
X
for which the space
C_{k}(X)
is separably quasi-complete, separably von Neumann complete or has ccp.

4
Convergent and summable sequences in topological vector spaces

Below we define (weakly) unconditionally Cauchy and unconditionally convergent series in a locally convex space naturally generalizing the corresponding notions in Banach spaces.

Definition 4.1

A series
\sum_{n\in\omega}x_{n}
in a separated topological vector spaces
E
is called

\bullet
unconditionally Cauchy (uC) if for every neighborhood
U
of zero there is an
N\in\omega
such that

\sum_{n\in F}x_{n}\in U
for every finite set
F\subseteq[N,\infty)
;

\bullet
weakly unconditionally Cauchy (wuC) if
\sum_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|
converges for every
\chi\in E^{\prime}
;

\bullet
unconditionally convergent (u.c.) if
\sum\lambda_{n}x_{n}
converges for all
(\lambda_{n})\in\ell_{\infty}
.∎

Now we extend the definitions of weakly
p
-summable, weakly
p
-convergent and weakly
p
-Cauchy sequences from the case of Banach spaces to the general case of separated topological vector spaces.

Definition 4.2

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
. A sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in a separated tvs
E
is called

\bullet

weakly
p
-summable if for every
\chi\in E^{\prime}
, it follows

(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle)_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{p}
if
p<\infty
, and
(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle)_{n\in\omega}\in c_{0}
if
p=\infty
;

\bullet

weakly
p
-convergent to
x\in E
if
\{x_{n}-x\}_{n\in\omega}
is weakly
p
-summable;

\bullet

weakly
p
-Cauchy if for each pair of strictly increasing sequences
(k_{n}),(j_{n})\subseteq\omega
, the sequence
(x_{k_{n}}-x_{j_{n}})_{n\in\omega}
is weakly
p
-summable.∎

If
1\leq p<q\leq\infty
and
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
is a sequence in a separated tvs
E
, Definitions
4.1
and
4.2
immediately imply the following relationships between the introduced notions

\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{ weakly $1$-summable =}\\
\mbox{ the series }\sum x_{n}\mbox{ is wuC}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{weakly}\\
\mbox{$p$-summable}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{weakly}\\
\mbox{$q$-summable}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{weakly $\infty$-summable}\\
\mbox{= weakly null}\end{subarray}}}

(4.1)

Notation 4.3

We shall denote by
\ell^{w}_{p}(E)
the family of all weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
. If
p=\infty
, for the simplicity of notations and formulations we shall usually identify
\ell^{w}_{\infty}(E)
with the space
c_{0}^{w}(E)
of all weakly null-sequences in
E
. Elements of
\ell^{w}_{p}(E)
will be written as
(x_{n})
. ∎

Example 4.4

Let
r,p\in[1,\infty]
, and let
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be the standard unit basis in
\ell_{r}
. Then
(e_{n})\in\ell_{p}^{w}(\ell_{r})
if and only if
p\geq r^{\ast}
.

Proof 16

Assume that
p\geq r^{\ast}
. If
\chi=(a_{n})\in\ell_{r^{\ast}}
, then
(\langle\chi,e_{n}\rangle)_{n\in\omega}=\chi\in\ell_{p}
. Therefore
(e_{n})\in\ell_{p}^{w}(\ell_{r})
. Assume that
p<r^{\ast}
. Take
\chi=(a_{n})\in\ell_{r^{\ast}}{\setminus}\ell_{p}
. Then
(\langle\chi,e_{n}\rangle)_{n\in\omega}=\chi\not\in\ell_{p}
and hence
(e_{n})\not\in\ell_{p}^{w}(\ell_{r})
.∎

We shall use the next standard lemma.

Lemma 4.5

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
, and let
E
be a locally convex space. If a weakly
p
-Cauchy sequence
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\omega}
has a subsequence
\{x_{k_{n}}\}_{n\in\omega}
which weakly
p
-converges to some
x\in E
, then also
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\omega}
weakly
p
-converges to
x
.

Proof 17

We prove the lemma only for the case
p<\infty
. Suppose for a contradiction that

\sum_{k\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{k}-x\rangle|^{p}=\infty\;\;\mbox{ for some }\;\chi\in E^{\prime}.

For every
n\in\omega
, let
j_{n}:=n
. Since
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\omega}
is weakly
p
-Cauchy we obtain

\infty=\Big(\sum_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{j_{n}}-x\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{p}\leq\Big(\sum_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{j_{n}}-x_{k_{n}}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{p}+\Big(\sum_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{k_{n}}-x\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{p}<\infty,

a contradiction. ∎
