is relatively compact in
\ell_{p}
.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i) follows from (ii) of Proposition
14.22
.∎

15
Gantmacher’s property for locally convex spaces and an operator characterization of Pełczyński’s property
sV_{p}

The aim of this section is to give an operator characterization of the property
sV_{p}
and an independent and short proof of Pełczyński’s Theorem
1.2
. First we recall some well known constructions, we use standard notations from
[
52
]
.

Let
E
be a locally convex space. Then every
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
defines a seminorm
q_{U}
by

q_{U}(x):=\inf\{\lambda>0:x\in\lambda U\}\quad(x\in E).

Set
N(U):=q_{U}^{-1}(0)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\tfrac{1}{n}U
. Observe that
N(U)
is a closed subspace of
E
such that
U=U+N(U)
.
Therefore
q_{U}(x)
defines a norm on the quotient vector space

E_{(U)}:=E/N(U)\quad\mbox{ by }\;\;\|x+N(U)\|_{U}:=q_{U}(x)\;\;(x\in E).

We denote by
\Phi_{U}
the quotient linear map
E\to E_{(U)}=E/N(U)
.
Observe that
U=\{x\in E:q_{U}(x)\leq 1\}
.
It follows that
\Phi_{U}(U)=B_{E_{(U)}}
and hence
\Phi_{U}
is continuous. Moreover, the co-restriction of the adjoint map
\Phi_{U}^{\ast}
onto
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}:=(E^{\prime})_{U^{\circ}}

which is also denoted by
\Phi_{U}^{\ast}
is an isometric isomorphism of
\big(E_{(U)}\big)^{\prime}_{\beta}
onto
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
. In particular,
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
is a Banach space.

The next assertion generalizes Proposition 17.1.2 of
[
52
]
and gives a characterization of compact-type operators defined in Definition
3.2
. Recall (see Section
3
) that the topology
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
of
E^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}:=(E^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}})
is the polar topology on
E^{\prime}
of uniform convergence on the members of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proposition 15.1

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces,
\mathcal{A}(L)\subseteq\mathsf{Bo}(L)
be a bornology on
L
, and let
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
. Consider the following assertions:

(i)

T\in\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
;

(ii)

there are
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and
T_{U}\in\mathcal{LA}(E_{(U)},L)\cap\mathcal{L}(E_{(U)},L_{T(U)})
such that
T=T_{U}\circ\Phi_{U}
;

(iii)

there is
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
such that the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}
maps
L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}
continuously into the Banach space
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
; in particular, the set
T^{\ast}\big(T(U)^{\circ}\big)\subseteq E^{\prime}
is equicontinuous.

Then
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
. The implication
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
is satisfied if
\mathcal{A}(L)
is saturated.

Proof 113

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Let
T\in\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
. Choose
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
such that
T(U)\in\mathcal{A}(L)
. Then
T(U)
is a bounded absolutely convex subset of
L
, and hence for every
x\in N(U)
, we obtain
T(x)\in\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\tfrac{1}{n}T(U)=\{0\}
. Therefore
T
induces a linear map
T_{U}:E_{(U)}\to L
.
Observe that
T_{U}
is continuous as a linear map from
E_{(U)}
into
L_{T(U)}
because
T_{U}\big(B_{E_{(U)}}\big)\subseteq T(U)
and
T(U)
is contained in the closed unit ball of
L_{T(U)}
. Since, by Proposition
3.6
, the norm topology of
L_{T(U)}
is finer than the induced original topology on
L
it follows that
T_{U}
is continuous. The inclusion
T_{U}\big(B_{E_{(U)}}\big)\subseteq T(U)
and the property (c) of the bornology
\mathcal{A}(L)
imply also that
T_{U}\in\mathcal{LA}(E_{(U)},L)
. It remains to note that
T=T_{U}\circ\Phi_{U}
.

The implication (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) immediately follows from the equality
T_{U}\big(B_{E_{(U)}}\big)=T(U)
(which holds because
\Phi_{U}(U)=B_{E_{(U)}}
).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) Assume that there are
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and an operator
T_{U}\in\mathcal{LA}(E_{(U)},L)
such that
T=T_{U}\circ\Phi_{U}
. Then
T^{\ast}=\Phi_{U}^{\ast}\circ T_{U}^{\ast}
. As we noticed above, the co-restriction of
\Phi_{U}^{\ast}
is an isometric isomorphism of
\big(E_{(U)}\big)^{\prime}_{\beta}
onto
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
. Observe that
B_{E_{(U)}}=U+N(U)
, where the right hand side contains conjugate classes of
x\in U
. Therefore, by the properties (b)-(c) of the bornology
\mathcal{A}(L)
and since
T_{U}\in\mathcal{LA}(E_{(U)},L)
, we obtain
T_{U}\big(U+N(U)\big)\in\mathcal{A}(L)
. Hence, by the definition of the topology
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
on
L^{\prime}
, the polar
W:=T_{U}\big(U+N(U)\big)^{\circ}
is a neighborhood of zero in
L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Now, for every
x\in U
and each
\chi\in W
, we obtain

|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle|=|\langle\chi,T_{U}\circ\Phi_{U}(x)\rangle|=|\langle\chi,T_{U}\big(x+N(U)\big)\rangle|\leq 1

which means that
T^{\ast}(W)\subseteq U^{\circ}=B_{E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}}
. Thus
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}\to E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
is continuous. Finally, since
T(U)^{\circ}=T_{U}\big(U+N(U)\big)^{\circ}=W
and
T^{\ast}\big(T(U)^{\circ}\big)=T^{\ast}(W)\subseteq U^{\circ}
the set
T^{\ast}\big(T(U)^{\circ}\big)
is equicontinuous.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Assume additionally that
\mathcal{A}(L)
is saturated, and let
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}\to E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
be continuous. Then there is
A\in\mathcal{A}
such that
T^{\ast}(A^{\circ})\subseteq U^{\circ}
. This means that for every
x\in U
and each
\chi\in A^{\circ}
, we have
|\langle\chi,T(x)\rangle|=|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle|\leq 1
and hence
T(U)\subseteq A^{\circ\circ}
. As
\mathcal{A}(L)
is saturated, we have
A^{\circ\circ}\in\mathcal{A}(L)
. Therefore, by (c) of the bornology
\mathcal{A}(L)
, it follows
T(U)\in\mathcal{A}(L)
. Thus
T\in\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
.∎

The next notion is motivated by (iii) of Proposition
15.1
.

Definition 15.2

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces. A linear mapping
T:L\to E^{\prime}
is called equicontinuous if for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(L)
, the set
T(U)\subseteq E^{\prime}
is equicontinuous.∎

Let
E
be a locally convex space. If
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
, then, by Theorem 11.3.5 of
[
67
]
,
U^{\circ}
is strongly bounded and hence, by Proposition
3.6
, the canonical inclusion
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is continuous. Therefore there exists a
finest
locally convex topology denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{n}
on the dual space
E^{\prime}
such that for every
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
, the canonical inclusion
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}\to(E^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{n})
is continuous. Clearly,
\beta(E^{\prime},E)\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{n}
. In the case when
E
is a normed space, the continuity of
E^{\prime}_{B_{E}^{\circ}}=E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to(E^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{n})
implies that
\mathcal{T}_{n}\subseteq\beta(E^{\prime},E)
, and hence
\mathcal{T}_{n}=\beta(E^{\prime},E)
is a Banach norm topology (which motivates the subscript
n
in the notation
\mathcal{T}_{n}
).

Theorem 15.3

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and assume that
\mathcal{A}(L)\subseteq\mathsf{Bo}(L)
is a saturated bornology on
L
. Then
\mathcal{L}(E,L)=\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
if and only if for every
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
, the adjoint mapping
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is equicontinuous and hence continuous.

Proof 114

Taking into account that
\beta(E^{\prime},E)\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{n}
, the necessity is proved in Proposition
15.1
. To prove the sufficiency, let
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
. Since
T^{\ast}
is equicontinuous, there are
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
such that
T^{\ast}(A^{\circ})\subseteq U^{\circ}
. This means that for every
x\in U
and each
\chi\in A^{\circ}
, we have
|\langle\chi,T(x)\rangle|=|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle|\leq 1
and hence
T(U)\subseteq A^{\circ\circ}
. As
\mathcal{A}(L)
is saturated, we have
A^{\circ\circ}\in\mathcal{A}(L)
. Therefore
T(U)\in\mathcal{A}(L)
and hence
T\in\mathcal{LA}(E,L)
.

To show that
T^{\ast}
is also continuous, fix a bounded subset
B
of
E
. As
T^{\ast}
is equicontinuous, there are
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
such that
T^{\ast}(A^{\circ})\subseteq U^{\circ}
. Since, by Theorem 11.3.5 of
[
67
]
,
U^{\circ}
is strongly bounded, there is
\lambda>0
such that
U^{\circ}\subseteq\lambda B^{\circ}
, and hence
T^{\ast}(\tfrac{1}{\lambda}A^{\circ})\subseteq\tfrac{1}{\lambda}U^{\circ}\subseteq B^{\circ}
. Thus
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is continuous.∎

Below we consider two cases when the adjoint operator has some of compact-type properties.
First we recall the next two well known and important results in Banach space theory.

Theorem 15.4
(Schauder)

Let
T:E\to L
be an operator between Banach spaces
E
and
L
. Then
T
is compact if and only if its adjoint
T^{\ast}
is compact.

Theorem 15.5
(Gantmacher)

Let
T:E\to L
be an operator between Banach spaces
E
and
L
. Then
T
is weakly compact if and only if its adjoint
T^{\ast}
is weakly compact.

The next proposition is a version of the “direct part” of Schauder’s Theorem
15.4
.

Proposition 15.6

Let
T
be a precompact operator from a locally convex space
E
to a normed space
L
. Then
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}})
is a compact subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, and hence the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is compact.

Proof 115

Consider the bornology
\mathcal{PC}(L)
of precompact subsets of
L
. Since
T
is precompact, Proposition
15.1
implies that for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, the map
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{PC}(L)}\to E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
is continuous. As
U^{\circ}
is strongly bounded by Theorem 11.3.5 of
[
67
]
, Proposition
3.6
implies that the norm topology of
E^{\prime}_{U^{\circ}}
is stronger than the topology induced from
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Therefore the map
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{PC}(L)}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is continuous. By the Alaoglu–Bourbaki Theorem 8.5.2 of
[
52
]
, the dual unit closed ball
B_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}=(B_{L})^{\circ}
is compact in
L^{\prime}_{\mathcal{PC}(L)}
. Thus
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}})
is a compact subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, as desired.∎

In what follows we shall use the following remark without mentioning.

Remark 15.7

Let
T
be an operator from an lcs
E
to a Banach space
L
. Then, by the (weak) angelicity of
L
, the operator
T
is (weakly) sequentially compact if and only if it is (weakly) compact.∎

The next lemma immediately follows from the corresponding definitions.

Lemma 15.8

Let
T
be an operator from a normed space
E
to a Banach space
L
. Then
T
is compact
(
resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact
)
if and only if so is its unique extension
\bar{T}:\overline{E}\to L
to the completion
\overline{E}
of
E
.

The following assertion is a version of the necessity in Gantmacher’s Theorem
15.5
.

Proposition 15.9

Let
T
be an operator from a locally convex space
E
to a Banach space
L
. If
T
is compact
(
resp., weakly compact
)
, then its adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is compact and sequentially compact
(
resp., weakly compact and weakly sequentially compact
)
.

Proof 116

Applying Proposition
15.1
to the family
\mathcal{A}(L)=\mathcal{RC}(L)
(resp.,
\mathcal{RWC}(L)
) we obtain that there are
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and an operator
T_{U}\in\mathcal{LA}(E_{(U)},L)
such that
T=T_{U}\circ\Phi_{U}
. Since
E_{(U)}
is a normed space, Lemma
15.8
implies that the extension
\overline{T_{U}}
of
T_{U}
onto the completion
\overline{E_{(U)}}
of
E_{(U)}
is a compact (resp., weakly compact) operator. Note also that
L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a Banach space and hence it is weakly angelic. Therefore, by the Schauder Theorem
15.4
and Gantmacher’s Theorem
15.5
, the adjoint operator
\overline{T_{U}}^{\,\ast}=T_{U}^{\ast}
is also compact and sequentially compact (resp., weakly compact and weakly sequentially compact), and hence so is
T^{\ast}=\Phi_{U}^{\ast}\circ\overline{T_{U}}^{\,\ast}
.∎

Using Proposition
15.9
we give below a sufficient condition on an lcs
E
to have the property
sV_{p}
, cf. Theorem
14.11
.

Proposition 15.10

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
, and let
E
be an
\ell_{\infty}
-
V_{p}
-barrelled space. If each
p
-convergent operator
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}
is weakly compact, then
E
has the property
sV_{p}
.

Proof 117

Let
B\subseteq E^{\prime}
be a
p
-
(V)
set. We prove that
B
is relatively weakly sequentially compact. To this end, fix an arbitrary sequence
S=\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
B
. By (iii) of Lemma
8.2
,
S
is also a
p
-
(V)
subset of
E^{\prime}
. Consider the linear map
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}
defined by

T(x):=\big(\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle\big)_{n\in\omega}\quad(x\in E).

Since
E
is
\ell_{\infty}
-
V_{p}
-barrelled, the sequence
S
is equicontinuous. Therefore, by (i) of Lemma
14.13
,
T
is continuous. As
S
is a a
p
-
(V)
set, (ii) of Lemma
14.13
implies that
T
is
p
-convergent. Hence, by assumption,
T
is weakly sequentially compact. By Proposition
15.9
, the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}:\ell_{\infty}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is also weakly sequentially compact.

For every
n\in\omega
, let
e_{n}^{\ast}
be the
n
th coordinate functional on
\ell_{\infty}
, and observe that
T^{\ast}(e_{n}^{\ast})=\chi_{n}
(indeed, if
x\in E
, then
\langle T^{\ast}(e_{n}^{\ast}),x\rangle=\langle e_{n}^{\ast},T(x)\rangle=\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle
). Since
T^{\ast}
is weakly sequentially compact and all
e_{n}^{\ast}
belong to the closed unit ball of
\ell_{\infty}^{\prime}
, there is a subsequence
\{e^{\ast}_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\omega}
of
\{e^{\ast}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
such that the sequence
\{T^{\ast}(e_{n_{k}}^{\ast})=\chi_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in\omega}
weakly converges to some functional
\chi\in E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Thus
B
is relatively weakly sequentially compact in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
.∎

Being motivated by Schauder’s Theorem
15.4
and Gantmacher’s Theorem
15.5
one can naturally introduce the following notions.

Definition 15.11

A locally convex space
E
is said to have

\bullet

the
(
sequential
)
Schauder property if for every Banach space
L
, an operator
T:E\to L
is compact if and only if its adjoint
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is (resp., sequentially) compact;

\bullet

the strong
(
sequential
)
Schauder property if for every lcs
L
, an operator
T:E\to L
is (resp., sequentially) compact if and only if so is its adjoint
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
;

\bullet

the
(
sequential
)
Gantmacher property if for every Banach space
L
, an operator
T:E\to L
is weakly compact if and only if
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is weakly (resp., sequentially) compact;

\bullet

the strong
(
sequential
)
Gantmacher property if for every lcs
L
, an operator
T:E\to L
is weakly (resp., sequentially) compact if and only if so is
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
.∎

The introduced notions are well-behaved under taking products.

Proposition 15.12

Let
\{E_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a non-empty family of locally convex spaces. Then the product
E=\prod_{i\in I}E_{i}
has the Schauder property
(
resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property
)
if and only if all
E_{i}
have the same property.

Proof 118

Assume that
E
has the Schauder property (resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property). Fix
j\in I
, and let
T_{j}:E_{j}\to L
be an operator from
E_{j}
to a Banach space
L
. If
T_{j}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact), then, by Proposition
15.9
, the adjoint operator
T_{j}^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to(E_{j})^{\prime}_{\beta}
has the same property. Conversely, assume that
T_{j}^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to(E_{j})^{\prime}_{\beta}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact). Let
0_{j}:\prod_{i\in I{\setminus}\{j\}}E_{i}\to L
be the zero operator, and let
I_{j}:(E_{j})^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
be the identity embedding onto the
j
coordinate. Define an operator
T:E\to L
by
T=T_{j}\times 0_{j}
. Then for every
(x_{i})\in E
and each
\chi\in L^{\prime}
, we have

\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),(x_{i})\rangle=\langle\chi,\big(T(x_{i})\big)_{i\in I}\rangle=\langle\chi,T_{j}(x_{j})\rangle=\langle T_{j}^{\ast}(\chi),x_{j}\rangle=\big\langle\big(I_{j}\circ T_{j}^{\ast}\big)(\chi),(x_{i})\big\rangle,

which means that
T^{\ast}=I_{j}\circ T_{j}^{\ast}
. Since
T_{j}^{\ast}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact) so is the operator
T^{\ast}
. By the Schauder property (resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property) of
E
, the operator
T=T_{j}\times 0_{j}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact), and hence so is
T_{j}
. Thus
E_{j}
has the Schauder property (resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property).

Assume now that all spaces
E_{i}
have the Schauder property (resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property). Let
T
be an operator from
E
to a Banach space
L
. If
T
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact), then, by Proposition
15.9
, so is also the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}
. Conversely, assume that
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact). By Lemma
2.6
, there is a finite subset
F
of
I
such that
\prod_{i\in I{\setminus}F}E_{i}
is in the kernel of
T
. Therefore to show that
T
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact) it suffices to assume that
I
has only two elements. So, consider an operator
T:E=H\times Z\to L
such that
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to H^{\prime}_{\beta}\times Z^{\prime}_{\beta}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact). Let
I_{H}:H\to H\times Z
and
I_{Z}:Z\to H\times Z
be the coordinate embeddings. Then

T(h,z)=T(h,0)+T(0,z)=T\circ I_{H}(h)+T\circ I_{Z}(z).

(15.1)

Since
T^{\ast}
is compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact) so are
I_{H}^{\ast}\circ T^{\ast}
and
I_{Z}^{\ast}\circ T^{\ast}
. Since
H
and
Z
have the Schauder property (resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property), it follows that the operators
T\circ I_{H}:H\to L
and
T\circ I_{Z}:Z\to L
are compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly compact or weakly sequentially compact). Thus, by (
15.1
), also the operator
T
has the same property. ∎

It is well known that any locally convex space embeds into the product of a family of Banach spaces. This fact and Schauder’s Theorem
15.4
, Gantmacher’s Theorem
15.5
and Proposition
15.12
immediately imply the following corollary.

Corollary 15.13

Any locally convex space embeds into a locally convex space with the Schauder property
(
resp., the sequential Schauder property, the Gantmacher property or the sequential Gantmacher property
)
.

Proposition 15.14

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
,
E
be a locally convex space with the sequential Gantmacher property, and let
\Gamma
be an infinite set. If
E
has the property
sV_{p}
, then each
p
-convergent operator
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
is weakly
(
sequentially
)
compact.

Proof 119

Let
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
be a
p
-convergent operator. To show that
T
is weakly sequentially compact, by the sequential Gantmacher property, it suffices to prove that the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}
is weakly sequentially compact. Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\omega}
be an arbitrary weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E
, and let
B
be the closed unit ball of
\ell^{\prime}_{\infty}(\Gamma)
. Since
T
is
p
-convergent,
T(x_{i})\to 0
in
\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
. Therefore

\sup_{\eta\in B}\big|\langle T^{\ast}(\eta),x_{i}\rangle\big|=\sup_{\eta\in B}\big|\langle\eta,T(x_{i})\rangle\big|\leq\|T(x_{i})\|\to 0,

which means that
T^{\ast}(B)
is a
p
-
(V)
set in
E^{\prime}
. By the property
sV_{p}
, we obtain that
T^{\ast}(B)
is a relatively weakly sequentially compact subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Thus
T^{\ast}
is weakly sequentially compact.∎

Joining Proposition
15.10
and Proposition
15.14
we obtain the following operator characterization of the property
sV_{p}
which generalizes Pełczyński’s operator characterization of the property
V
for Banach spaces, see
[
71
]
.

Theorem 15.15

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
, and let
E
be an
\ell_{\infty}
-
V_{p}
-barrelled space with the sequential Gantmacher property. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

for every Banach space
L
, each
p
-convergent operator
T:E\to L
is weakly compact;

(ii)

each
p
-convergent operator
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}
is weakly compact;

(iii)

E
has the property
sV_{p}
.

Proof 120

The implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is trivial, and (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) follows from Proposition
15.10
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) It is well known that there is an infinite set
\Gamma
such that the Banach space
L
embeds into
\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
. Let
R:L\to\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
be an embedding. Then
R\circ T
is also a
p
-convergent operator. Therefore, by Proposition
15.14
,
R\circ T
is weakly compact. Since
R(L)
is a closed subspace of
\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
, it is also weakly closed. It follows that if
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}(E)
is such that
R\circ T(U)
is relatively weakly compact in
\ell_{\infty}(\Gamma)
, then
T(U)
is relatively weakly compact in
L
. Thus
T
is a weakly compact operator.∎

Below we provide a short proof of Pełczyński’s Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 15.16
(
[
71
]
)

For every
p\in[1,\infty]
and each compact space
K
, the Banach space
C(K)
has the property
V_{p}
.

Proof 121

By Gantmacher’s Theorem
15.5
and Theorem
15.15
, it suffice to prove that each
p
-convergent operator
T:C(K)\to\ell_{\infty}
is weakly compact. Suppose for a contradiction that there is a
p
-convergent operator
T:C(K)\to\ell_{\infty}
which is not weakly compact. Since
T
is not weakly compact, Pełczyński’s Theorem 5.5.3 of
[
1
]
implies that
C(K)
has a subspace
H
isomorphic to
c_{0}
such that the restriction
T{\restriction}_{H}
is an isomorphism. Identifying
H
with
c_{0}
, we observe that the standard unit basis
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
of
c_{0}
is weakly
p
-summable (indeed, if
x=(a_{n})\in\ell_{1}
, then
\sum_{n\in\omega}\big|\langle x,e_{n}\rangle\big|^{p}=\sum_{n\in\omega}|a_{n}|^{p}<\infty
). Since
e_{n}\not\to 0
in
c_{0}
and
T{\restriction}_{H}
is an isomorphism, it follows that
T(e_{n})\not\to 0
in
L
. Hence
T
is not
p
-convergent, a contradiction.∎

16
(q,p)
-convergent operators and characterizations of Pełczyński’s type sets

In Section
14
we obtained operator characterizations of
p
-
(V^{\ast})
sets and
p
-
(V)
sets using weakly sequentially
p
-precompact operators, see Theorems
14.16
and
14.23
. The purpose of this section is to find operator characterizations of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets and
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets. For this we recall first some definitions.

Let
L
be a Banach space and
p\in[1,\infty]
. Recall that a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
L
is called
strongly
p
-summable
if
\big(\|x_{n}\|)\in\ell_{p}
(or
\big(\|x_{n}\|)\in c_{0}
if
p=\infty
). The family of all strongly
p
-summable sequences in
L
is denoted by
\ell_{p}^{s}(L)
(or by
c_{0}^{s}(L)
if
p=\infty
). Recall also that
\ell_{p}^{s}(L)
and
c_{0}^{s}(L)
are Banach spaces under pointwise operations and the norm defined by

\big\|(x_{n})\big\|^{strong}_{p}:=\Big(\sum_{n\in\omega}\|x_{n}\|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\big\|(x_{n})\big\|^{strong}_{\infty}:=\sup_{n\in\omega}\big\|x_{n}\big\|,

respectively.

The notion of strongly
p
-summable sequences in Banach spaces can be naturally extended to the general case as follows, see § 19.4 of
[
52
]
: a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in an lcs
L
is called
strongly
p
-summable
if
\big(q_{U}(x_{n})\big)\in\ell_{p}
(or
\big(q_{U}(x_{n})\big)\in c_{0}
if
p=\infty
) for every
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, where as usual
q_{U}
denotes the gauge functional of
U
.
Since
U\subseteq V
implies
q_{U}\geq q_{V}
, for Banach spaces, this notion of strong
p
-summability coincides with the above mentioned usual notion.

Below we introduce a new class of operators which allows to characterize
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets and
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets.

Definition 16.1

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
, and let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces. A linear map
T:E\to L
is called
(q,p)
-convergent if it sends weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
to strongly
q
-summable sequences in
L
. ∎

Remark 16.2

(i) Our terminology is based on the evident fact that an operator
T:E\to L
is
(\infty,p)
-convergent if and only if it is
p
-convergent and the important notion of
(q,p)
-summing operators between Banach spaces (see
[
24
]
,
[
75
]
and Section
17
below).

(ii) The condition
p\leq q
in Definition
16.1
is important because if
q<p
, then only the zero linear map can be
(q,p)
-convergent. Indeed, if
T\not=0
, take
(\lambda_{n})\in\ell_{p}{\setminus}\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}{\setminus}\ell_{q}
if
p=\infty
) and
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
such that
q_{U}\big(T(x)\big)\not=0
for some
x\in E
. Then the sequence
\{x_{n}=\lambda_{n}x\}_{n\in\omega}
is weakly
p
-summable. However, the series
\sum_{i\in\omega}q_{U}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}=q_{U}\big(T(x)\big)^{q}\sum_{i\in\omega}|\lambda_{n}|^{q}
diverges.∎

Using
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets we can characterize
(q,p)
-convergent operators into normed spaces as follows.

Theorem 16.3

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space, and let
T
be an operator from
E
to a normed space
L
. Then
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent if and only if
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
subset of
E^{\prime}
.

Proof 122

First we observe that for every
x\in E
, we have

\|T(x)\|=\sup_{\chi\in B_{L^{\prime}}}|\langle\chi,T(x)\rangle|=\sup_{\chi\in B_{L^{\prime}}}|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle|.

Now, for every weakly
p
-summable sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
, we obtain that
\big(\|T(x_{n})\|)_{n}\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
) if and only if

\Big(\sup_{\chi\in B_{L^{\prime}}}|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),x_{n}\rangle|\Big)\in\ell_{q}\;\;\;\;\big(\mbox{or }\in c_{0}\;\mbox{ if $q=\infty$}\big).

Taking into account that
B_{L^{\prime}}
and hence also
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
are equicontinuous, it follows that
\big(\|T(x_{n})\|)_{n}\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
) if and only if
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
subset of
E^{\prime}
.∎

The next corollary complements Corollary
14.7
.

Corollary 16.4

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space with the property
EV_{(p,q)}

(
resp., the property
sEV_{(p,q)}
)
, and let
T
be an operator from
E
to a normed space
L
. If
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent, then
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is weakly
(
resp., sequentially
)
compact. The converse is true if every weakly compact, absolutely convex and equicontinuous subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
set.

Proof 123

By Theorem
16.3
, the absolutely convex set
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
subset of
E^{\prime}
. Therefore, by the property
EV_{(p,q)}
(resp., the property
sEV_{(p,q)}
),
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a relatively weakly (resp., sequentially) compact subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, as desired.

Conversely, assume that every weakly compact, absolutely convex and equicontinuous subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
set and
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a relatively weakly (resp., sequentially) compact subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Since
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is also equicontinuous we obtain that its closure
\mathrm{cl}_{E^{\prime}_{\beta}}\big(T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})\big)
and hence also
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
are
(p,q)
-
(EV)
sets. Thus, by Theorem
16.3
,
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent.∎

The following assertion is dual to Theorem
16.3
.

Theorem 16.5

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space, and let
T
be an operator from a normed space
L
to
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Then
T(B_{L})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V)
subset of
E^{\prime}
if and only if the linear map
T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}
from
E
to
L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

Proof 124

First we note that for every
x\in E
, we have (recall that
J_{E}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
denotes the canonical map)

\|T^{\ast}(x)\|_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}=\|T^{\ast}(J_{E}(x))\|_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}=\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle T^{\ast}(J_{E}(x)),y\rangle|=\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle T(y),x\rangle|.

(16.1)

Let now
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E
. By (
16.1
), we have

\big(\|T^{\ast}(x_{n})\|_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}\big)_{n\in\omega}=\big(\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle T(y),x_{n}\rangle|\big)_{n\in\omega}
. Then the theorem follows from the definition of
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets and the definition of
(q,p)
-convergent linear maps.∎

The next theorem characterizes barrelled lcs for which
(p,q)
-
(V)
subsets of
E^{\prime}
have additional compact-type topological property.

Theorem 16.6

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
, let
E
be a
(
resp., an
\aleph_{0}
-barrelled
)
locally convex space. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

if
L
is a normed space and
T:E\to L
is a
(q,p)
-convergent operator, then
T^{\ast}:L^{\prime}_{\beta}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
;

(ii)

the same as
(i)
with
L=\ell_{\infty}
;

(iii)

each
(p,q)
-
(EV)

(
resp.,
(p,q)
-
(V)
)
subset of
E^{\prime}
is relatively weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
.

Proof 125

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is clear.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) Let
B
be a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
(resp.,
(p,q)
-
(V)
) subset of
E^{\prime}
. Fix an arbitrary sequence
S=\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
B
. We claim that
S
is equicontinuous. This clear if
B
be a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
set. Assume that
E
is
\aleph_{0}
-barrelled. Since
B
is weak
∗
bounded (see (ii) of Lemma
8.2
), the
\aleph_{0}
-barrelleddeness of
E
implies that
S
is equicontinuous. Therefore we can apply Lemma
14.13
to get that the linear map
T:E\to\ell_{\infty}
defined by

T(x):=\big(\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle\big)_{n\in\omega}\quad(x\in E).

is continuous.

Let now
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E
. Since
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
is also a
(p,q)
-
(V)
set, we obtain

\big(\sup_{i\in\omega}|\langle\chi_{i},x_{n}\rangle|\big)_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{q}\quad(\mbox{or $\in c_{0}$ if $q=\infty$}).

(16.2)

Observe that
\|T(x_{n})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=\sup_{i\in\omega}|\langle\chi_{i},x_{n}\rangle|
for every
n\in\omega
. Therefore (
16.2
) implies

\big(\|T(x_{n})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\big)_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{q}\quad(\mbox{or $\in c_{0}$ if $q=\infty$}),

which means that
T
is a
(q,p)
-convergent operator. Therefore, by (ii), the adjoint operator
T^{\ast}
is weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact).

For every
n\in\omega
, if
e_{n}^{\ast}
is the
n
th coordinate functional of
\ell_{\infty}
and
x\in E
, then
\langle T^{\ast}(e_{n}^{\ast}),x\rangle=\langle e_{n}^{\ast},T(x)\rangle=\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle
and hence
T^{\ast}(e_{n}^{\ast})=\chi_{n}
. Therefore the sequence
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
has a subsequence which weakly converges (resp., converges, weakly sequentially
p
-converges or weakly sequentially
p
-Cauchy). Thus
B
is relatively weakly sequentially compact (resp.,
relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact).

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Let
T:E\to L
be a
(q,p)
-convergent operator to a normed space
L
. Then, by Theorem
16.3
,
T^{\ast}(B_{L^{\prime}})
is a
(p,q)
-
(EV)
subset of
E^{\prime}
(and hence also a
(p,q)
-
(V)
set). Now (iii) implies that
T^{\ast}
is a weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact) operator.∎

Theorem 16.7

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
, let
E
be a locally convex space. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

if
L
is a normed space and
T:L\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is an operator such that
T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}:E\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(q,p)
-convergent linear map, then
T
is weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
;

(ii)

the same as
(i)
with
L=\ell_{1}^{0}
;

(iii)

each bounded subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
which is a
(p,q)
-
(V)
set is relatively weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
.

Moreover, if
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is locally complete, then
(i)-(iii)
are equivalent to

(iv)

the same as
(i)
with
L=\ell_{1}
.

Proof 126

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (i)
\Rightarrow
(iv) are clear.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) and (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Let
B
be a bounded
(p,q)
-
(V)
subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Fix an arbitrary sequence
S=\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
B
, so
S
is a bounded subset of
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Therefore, by Proposition
14.9
, the linear map
T:\ell_{1}^{0}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
(or
T:\ell_{1}\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
if
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
is locally complete) defined by

T(a_{0}e_{0}+\cdots+a_{n}e_{n}):=a_{0}\chi_{0}+\cdots+a_{n}\chi_{n}\quad(n\in\omega,\;a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{F}).

is continuous. For every
n\in\omega
and each
x\in E
, we have

\langle T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}(x),e_{n}\rangle=\langle T(e_{n}),x\rangle=\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle

and hence
T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}(x)=\big(\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle\big)_{n}\in\ell_{\infty}
. In particular,
\|T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}(x)\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=\sup_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi_{n},x\rangle|
.

Let now
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E
. Since
B
and hence also
S
are
(p,q)
-
(V)
sets we obtain
\big(\|T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}(x_{n})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\big)=\big(\sup_{i\in\omega}|\langle\chi_{i},x_{n}\rangle|\big)\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
). Therefore
T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}
is
(q,p)
-convergent, and hence, by (ii) or (iv),
T
is weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact). Therefore
S
has a weakly convergent (resp., convergent, weakly
p
-convergent, or weakly
p
-Cauchy) subsequence, as desired.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Let
T:L\to E^{\prime}_{\beta}
be an operator from a normed space such that
T^{\ast}{\restriction}_{E}:E\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(q,p)
-convergent linear map. Then, by Theorem
16.5
,
T(B_{L})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V)
set and hence it is relatively weakly sequentially compact (resp., relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact). Thus
T
is weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact).∎

We need the following result which is similar to Theorem
16.5
.

Theorem 16.8

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space, and let
T
be an operator from a normed space
L
to
E
. Then
T(B_{L})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subset of
E
if and only if
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

Proof 127

First we note that for every
\chi\in E^{\prime}
, we have

\|T^{\ast}(\chi)\|_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}=\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),y\rangle|=\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle\chi,T(y)\rangle|.

(16.3)

Let
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Then, by (
16.3
), we have

\big(\|T^{\ast}(\chi_{n})\|_{L^{\prime}_{\beta}}\big)_{n\in\omega}=\big(\sup_{y\in B_{L}}|\langle\chi_{n},T(y)\rangle|\big)_{n\in\omega}
. Now the theorem follows from the definition of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets and the definition of
(q,p)
-convergent operators.∎

Theorem 16.9

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
, and let
E
be a locally convex space. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

if
L
is a normed space and
T:L\to E
is an operator such that
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is
(q,p)
-convergent, then
T
is weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
;

(ii)

the same as
(i)
with
L=\ell_{1}^{0}
;

(iii)

each
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subset of
E
is relatively weakly sequentially compact
(
resp., relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact
)
.

Moreover, if
E
is locally complete, then
(i)-(iii)
are equivalent to

(iv)

the same as
(i)
with
L=\ell_{1}
.

Proof 128

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (i)
\Rightarrow
(iv) are clear.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) and (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Let
A
be a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subset of
E
. Fix an arbitrary sequence
S=\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
A
, so
S
is a bounded subset of
E
. Therefore, by Proposition
14.9
, the linear map
T:\ell_{1}^{0}\to E
(or
T:\ell_{1}\to E
if
E
is locally complete) defined by

T(a_{0}e_{0}+\cdots+a_{n}e_{n}):=a_{0}x_{0}+\cdots+a_{n}x_{n}\quad(n\in\omega,\;a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{F}).

is continuous. For every
n\in\omega
and each
\chi\in E^{\prime}
, we have

\langle T^{\ast}(\chi),e_{n}\rangle=\langle\chi,T(e_{n})\rangle=\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle

and hence
T^{\ast}(\chi)=\big(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle\big)_{n}\in\ell_{\infty}
. In particular,
\|T^{\ast}(\chi)\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=\sup_{n\in\omega}|\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle|
.

Let now
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Since
A
and hence also
S
are
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets we obtain
\big(\|T^{\ast}(\chi_{n})\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\big)=\big(\sup_{i\in\omega}|\langle\chi_{n},x_{i}\rangle|\big)\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
). Therefore
T^{\ast}
is
(q,p)
-convergent, and hence, by (ii) or (iv),
T
is weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact). Therefore
S=\{T(e_{n})\}_{n\in\omega}
has a weakly convergent (resp., convergent, weakly
p
-convergent, or weakly
p
-Cauchy) subsequence, as desired.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Let
T:L\to E
be an operator from a normed space such that
T^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to L^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a
(q,p)
-convergent operator. Then, by Theorem
16.8
,
T(B_{L})
is a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
set and hence it is relatively weakly sequentially compact (resp., relatively sequentially compact, relatively weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact). Thus
T
is weakly sequentially compact (resp., sequentially compact, weakly sequentially
p
-compact or weakly sequentially
p
-precompact).∎

To obtain another characterization of
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
sets we need the next assertion.
For a set
A
, we denote by
\ell^{0}_{p}(A)
the subspace of the Banach space
\ell_{p}(A)
consisting of all vectors with finite support.

Proposition 16.10

Let
A
be a bounded subset of a locally convex space
(E,\tau)
,
B=\overline{\mathrm{acx}}(A)
, and let
i_{B}:(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)\to E
be the identity operator. Let
T_{A}:\ell_{1}^{0}(A)\to(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
be the identity inclusion defined by

T_{A}(\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n}):=\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n}\quad(n\in\omega,\;\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{F},\;a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in A).

Then
T_{A}
is bounded. Moreover, if
E
is locally complete, then
T_{A}
can be extended to a continuous operator
{\tilde{T}}_{A}
from
\ell_{1}(A)
to
(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
.
In particular,
i_{B}\circ T_{A}:\ell_{1}^{0}(A)\to E

(
or
i_{B}\circ{\tilde{T}}_{A}:\ell_{1}(A)\to E
if
E
is locally complete
)
is bounded and hence continuous.

Proof 129

First we recall that, by Proposition
3.6
,
i_{B}
is indeed an operator.

By the definition of
(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
we observe that
\|b\|\leq 1
for every
b\in B
. Therefore, if
|\lambda_{0}|+\cdots+|\lambda_{n}|\leq 1
and
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in A
, we obtain

\big\|T_{A}(\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n})\big\|=\|\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n}\|\leq 1

and hence
T:\ell_{1}^{0}(A)\to(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
is bounded.

Assume that
E
is locally complete. Then, by Proposition 5.1.6 of
[
72
]
, the space
E_{B}
is a Banach space. Therefore the operator
T:\ell_{1}^{0}(A)\to(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
can be extended to an operator
{\tilde{T}}_{A}:\ell_{1}(A)\to(E_{B},\|\cdot\|)
. ∎

Theorem 16.11

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
. Then a subset
A
of a locally convex space
E
is a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
set if and only if the adjoint operator
T_{A}^{\ast}:E^{\prime}_{\beta}\to\ell_{\infty}(A)
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

Proof 130

By Proposition
16.10
, every bounded subset
A
of
E
defines an operator
T_{A}:\ell_{1}^{0}(A)\to E
by

T_{A}(\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n}):=\lambda_{0}a_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}a_{n}\quad(n\in\omega,\;\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{F},\;a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in A).

Observe that for each
\chi\in E^{\prime}
, the
a
th coordinate
T_{A}^{\ast}(\chi)(a)
of
T_{A}^{\ast}(\chi)
is

T_{A}^{\ast}(\chi)(a)=\langle T_{A}^{\ast}(\chi),a\rangle=\langle\chi,T_{A}(a)\rangle=\langle\chi,a\rangle,

and hence

\|T^{\ast}_{A}(\chi)\|_{\ell_{\infty}(A)}=\sup_{a\in A}|T^{\ast}_{A}(\chi)(a)|=\sup_{a\in A}|\langle\chi,a\rangle|

(16.4)

Now, by definition, a subset
A
of
E
is a
(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
set if and only if
\big(\sup_{a\in A}|\langle\chi_{n},a\rangle|\big)\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
) for every weakly
p
-summable sequence
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, and hence, by (
16.4
), if and only if
\big(\|T^{\ast}_{A}(\chi_{n})\|_{\ell_{\infty}(A)}\big)\in\ell_{q}
(or
\in c_{0}
if
q=\infty
) for every weakly
p
-summable sequence
\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E^{\prime}_{\beta}
, i.e.,
T^{\ast}_{A}
is a
(q,p)
-convergent operator.∎

17
(q,p)
-summing operators and applications

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
.
Recall (see Definition
16.1
) that an operator
T
from an lcs
E
to an lcs
L
is
(q,p)
-convergent
if it sends weakly
p
-summable sequences in
E
to strongly
q
-summable sequences in
L
. Using this class of operators we characterized in Theorem
16.11

(p,q)
-
(V^{\ast})
subsets of
E
. Therefore it is natural to find some classes of operators from
E
to
L
which are
(q,p)
-convergent. If
E
and
L
are Banach spaces it is well known (see
[
24
]
) that the class of
(q,p)
-convergent operators coincides with the class of
(q,p)
-summing operators (a detailed proof of a more general result is given in Corollary
17.7
below). In this section we define
(q,p)
-summing operators between arbitrary locally convex spaces, show that the most important results for Banach spaces (as the Pietsch Domination Theorem and the Pietsch factorization Theorem) remain true and show that any
(q,p)
-summing operator is
(q,p)
-convergent.

First we recall the definition of
(q,p)
-summing operators between Banach spaces which plays an essential role in many areas of Banach Space Theory, see the classical books
[
24
]
and
[
62
]
.

Definition 17.1

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
. An operator
T:X\to Y
between Banach spaces is called
(q,p)
-summing if there is a constant
C>0
such that for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in X
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in B_{X^{\prime}}\Big\}.

(17.1)

The least
C
for which the inequality (
17.1
) always holds is denoted by
\pi_{q,p}(T)
. We shall write
\prod_{q,p}(X,Y)
for the family of all
(q,p)
-summing operators from
X
into
Y
. If
q=p
we shall say that
T
is
p
-summing and set
\prod_{p}(X,Y):=\prod_{p,p}(X,Y)
and
\pi_{p}(T)=\pi_{p,p}(T)
.∎

Since the both sides in (
17.1
) are homogenous, one can assume that all vectors
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
belongs to the closed unit ball
B_{X}
of
X
. Observe also that
B_{X}
is an absolutely convex closed neighborhood of
0\in X
(that is
B_{X}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(X)
) and
B_{X^{\prime}}=B_{X}^{\circ}
. Therefore the notion of
(q,p)
-summing operator between Banach spaces is defined by the very specific neighborhood
B_{X}
of zero (or by the dual pair
\big(B_{X},B_{X}^{\circ}\big)
).
Taking into account these remarks one can naturally define
(q,p)
-summing operators between locally convex spaces as follows.

Definition 17.2

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
. An operator
T:E\to L
is called
(q,p)
-summing with respect to
U
or
U
-
(q,p)
-summing if for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
there is a constant
C_{V}>0
such that for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\},

(17.2)

where
q_{V}
is the gauge of
V
. The least
C_{V}
for which the inequality (
17.2
) always holds is denoted by
\pi_{q,p}^{U,V}(T)
. We denote by
\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
the family of all
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operators from
E
to
L
. If
q=p
we shall say that
T
is
U
-
p
-summing and set
\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L):=\prod_{p,p}^{U}(E,L)
and
\pi_{p}^{U,V}(T):=\pi_{p,p}^{U,V}(T)
.∎

In the case when
L
is a Banach space Definition
17.2
can be formulated in a simpler form (this follows from that fact that if
V\subseteq V^{\prime}
, then
q_{V}\geq q_{V^{\prime}}
).

Definition 17.3

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space,
L
be a Banach space, and let
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
. An operator
T:E\to L
is called
(q,p)
-summing with respect to
U
or
U
-
(q,p)
-summing if there is a constant
C>0
such that for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

(17.3)

The least
C
for which the inequality (
17.3
) always holds is denoted by
\pi_{q,p}^{U}(T)
. We denote by
\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
the family of all
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operators from
E
to
L
. If
q=p
we shall say that
T
is
U
-
p
-summing and set
\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L):=\prod_{p,p}^{U}(E,L)
and
\pi_{p}^{U}(T):=\pi_{p,p}^{U}(T)
.∎

We emphasize that the notion of
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operators indeed depends on
U
(or, more precisely, on the dual pair
(U,U^{\circ})
) even for Banach spaces as the following example shows.

Example 17.4

Let
1\leq p<\infty
, and let
(\lambda_{n})\in\ell_{2}
be such that
0<\lambda_{n}<1
for every
n\in\omega
. Let
E=L=\ell_{2}
, and let
U:=E\cap\prod_{n\in\omega}\big[-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}},\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\big]
. Then the diagonal operator

D_{\lambda}:E\to E\mbox{ defined by }D_{\lambda}(a_{n}):=(\lambda_{n}a_{n})

is
B_{E}
-
p
-summing
(
i.e.,
D_{\lambda}
is
p
-summing in the sense of Definition
17.1
)
but it is not
U
-
p
-summing.

Proof 131

The operator
D_{\lambda}
is
B_{E}
-
p
-summing by Theorem 2.b.4 of
[
62
]
. Observe that
\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}\in U
for all
k\in\omega
, where
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
denotes the standard unit basis of
\ell_{2}
. It is clear that if
\chi=(b_{n})\in U^{\circ}
, then
|b_{n}|\leq\lambda_{n}^{2}
. Therefore the right hand side of (
17.3
), applied to
n=0
and
x_{0}^{k}=\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}
, is equal to

C\cdot\sup\{|\langle\chi,x_{0}^{k}\rangle|:\chi\in U^{\circ}\}\leq C\cdot|\lambda_{k}|\to 0\;\;(k\to\infty).

On the other hand, since
\|D_{\lambda}(x_{0}^{k})\|=\|e_{k}\|=1
for every
k\in\omega
, we see that the operator
D_{\lambda}
is not
U
-
p
-summing.∎

Taking into consideration Theorem
16.11
, the case when
L
is a Banach space is of independent interest.
Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space,
L
be a Banach space, and let
T:E\to L
be an operator. Then the
associative linear map

{\hat{T}}:\ell_{p}[E]\to\ell_{q}^{s}(L)
is defined by

{\hat{T}}\big((y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big):=\big((T(y_{n}))_{n\in\omega}\big),\quad(y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{p}[E].

The question when
{\hat{T}}
is well-defined is considered in (i) of Proposition
17.5
below.

Let
E
be a Banach space. Then Proposition 2.1 of
[
24
]
states that if
p=q
, then
T
is
p
-summing if and only if
{\hat{T}}
is continuous, and Proposition 17.2.3 of
[
75
]
states that
T
is
(q,p)
-summing if and only if it is
(q,p)
-convergent. Below we extend these results. Recall that
for every lcs
E
and
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, the seminorm
\rho_{U}:\ell_{p}^{w}(E)\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by
\rho_{U}\big((x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big):=\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\big\|\big(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle\big)_{n}\big\|_{\ell_{p}}
.

Proposition 17.5

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space,
L
be a Banach space, and let
T:E\to L
be an operator.

(i)

T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
if and only if
{\hat{T}}
is well-defined and continuous.

(ii)

If
{\hat{T}}
is continuous, then
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

(iii)

If the space
\ell_{p}[E]
is barrelled and
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent, then
{\hat{T}}
is continuous.

Proof 132

(i) Assume that
T
is a
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operator for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
. To prove that
{\hat{T}}
is well-defined and continuous it suffices to show that

\big\|\big((T(y_{n}))_{n\in\omega}\big)\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq\pi_{q,p}^{U}

for every
(y_{n})\in\ell_{p}[E]
such that
\rho_{U}\big((y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)\leq 1
.

Now, let
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
be a weakly
p
-summable sequence in
E
such that
\rho_{U}\big((x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)\leq 1
. Since
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing, for every
n\in\omega
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq\pi_{q,p}^{U}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

(17.4)

Then

\displaystyle\big\|\big(T(x_{n})\big)_{n\in\omega}\|_{\ell_{q}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\omega}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq\pi_{q,p}^{U}\cdot\sup_{n\in\omega}\;\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

(17.5)

\displaystyle=\pi_{q,p}^{U}\cdot\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\;\sup_{n\in\omega}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}=\pi_{q,p}^{U}\cdot\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\big\|\big(\langle\chi,x_{n}\rangle\big)_{n}\big\|_{\ell_{p}}\leq\pi_{q,p}^{U},

as desired.

Conversely, assume that
{\hat{T}}
is well-defined and continuous. Choose
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and
C>0
such that

\big\|\big(T(y_{n})\big)_{n\in\omega}\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq C\;\;\mbox{
for every $(y_{n})\in\ell_{p}[E]$ with $\rho_{U}\big((y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)\leq 1$.}

(17.6)

We show that
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{U}\leq C
. To this end, let
F=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq U
be arbitrary. For every
i>n
, set
x_{i}:=0
. Then
(x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{p}[E]
. Set
a:=\rho_{U}\big((x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)
and consider two possible cases.

Case 1. Assume that
a=0
. By the definition of
\rho_{U}
this means that
\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle=0
for every
0\leq i\leq n
and each
\chi\in U^{\circ}
. Therefore
\mathsf{span}(F)\subseteq U^{\circ\circ}=U
and
\rho_{U}\big((b_{n}x_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)=0
for each sequence
(b_{n})\in\mathbb{F}^{\omega}
. Then (
17.6
) implies that
F\subseteq\ker(T)
, and hence

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}=0=C\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Case 2. Assume that
a>0
. Then
\rho_{U}\big((\tfrac{x_{n}}{a})_{n\in\omega}\big)=1
and hence, by (
17.6
), we obtain

\tfrac{1}{a}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}=\big\|\big(T(\tfrac{x_{n}}{a})\big)_{n\in\omega}\big\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq C,

or

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\|T(x_{i})\|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C\cdot a=C\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Cases 1 and 2 show that
T
is a
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operator with
\pi_{q,p}^{U}\leq C
.

(ii) Assume that
{\hat{T}}
is continuous. Choose
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and
C>0
such that

\big\|\big(T(y_{n})\big)_{n\in\omega}\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq C\;\;\mbox{
for every $(y_{n})\in\ell_{p}[E]$ with $\rho_{U}\big((y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)\leq 1$.}

To show that
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent, take an arbitrary weakly
p
-summable sequence
S=\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
in
E
. Since
S
is bounded, there is
\lambda>0
such that
S\subseteq\lambda U
. Then, by Cases 1 and 2 in the proof of Lemma
4.7
, we obtain
\rho_{U}\big(S)\leq\lambda
. Therefore
\big\|\big(T(y_{n})\big)_{n\in\omega}\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq C\lambda
. Thus
T
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

(iii) Let
\ell_{p}[E]
be a barrelled space, and assume that
T
is a
(q,p)
-convergent operator. Then we can define a linear map
{\hat{T}}:\ell_{p}[E]\to\ell_{q}^{s}(L)
by

{\hat{T}}\big((y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big):=\big(T(y_{n})\big)_{n\in\omega},\quad(y_{n})_{n\in\omega}\in\ell_{p}[E].

Observe that
{\hat{T}}
has a closed graph
\Gamma_{{\hat{T}}}
because if a net
\big((y_{n}^{\alpha})_{n\in\omega},(T(y_{n}^{\alpha}))_{n\in\omega}\big)
converges to a point
\big((z_{n})_{n\in\omega},(t_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)\in\ell_{p}[E]\times\ell_{p}^{s}(L)
, then:

(1)

(y_{n}^{\alpha})_{n\in\omega}\to(z_{n})_{n\in\omega}
in
\ell_{p}[E]
and hence
y_{n}^{\alpha}\to z_{n}
in
E
for every
n\in\omega
, and

(2)

(T(y_{n}^{\alpha}))_{n\in\omega}\to(t_{n})_{n\in\omega}
in the Banach space
\ell_{p}^{s}(L)
and hence
T(y_{n}^{\alpha})\to t_{n}
in
L
for every
n\in\omega
.

Therefore
t_{n}=T(z_{n})
for every
n\in\omega
, and hence
\big((z_{n})_{n\in\omega},(t_{n})_{n\in\omega}\big)=\big((z_{n})_{n\in\omega},(T(z_{n}))_{n\in\omega}\big)
. Thus
\Gamma_{{\hat{T}}}
is closed.

Since the space
\ell_{p}[E]
is barrelled, the Closed Graph Theorem 14.3.4 of
[
67
]
implies that
{\hat{T}}
is continuous.∎

Remark 17.6

If
q<p
, then, by Remark
16.2
, only the zero linear map is
(q,p)
-convergent. Therefore, by Proposition
17.5
, there is sense to consider
(q,p)
-summable operators only if
q\geq p
.∎

Since, by Corollary
4.12
,
\ell_{p}[E]
is a Fréchet space (hence barrelled) if so is
E
, the next result immediately follows from Proposition
17.5
.

Corollary 17.7

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
be a locally convex space,
L
be Banach spaces, and let
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,L)
. If the space
\ell_{p}[E]
is barrelled
(
if, for example,
E
is a Fréchet space
)
, then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summable for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
;

(ii)

{\hat{T}}:\ell_{p}[E]\to\ell_{q}^{s}(L)
is continuous;

(iii)

T
is
(q,p)
-convergent.

In spite of Example
17.4
, we show below that the most important general results concerning
(q,p)
-summing operators proved in Sections 2 and 10 of
[
24
]
remain true also in the general case of Definition
17.2
and Definition
17.3
. We start from the following extension of Proposition 2.3 of
[
24
]
which states that every finite rank operator between Banach spaces is
p
-summing.
Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
H
be a subspace of
E^{\prime}
. We shall say that a finite rank operator
T(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\langle\eta_{i},x\rangle\cdot y_{i}
from
E
to
L

has co-support in
H
if
\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{n}\in H
.

Proposition 17.8

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
. Then:

(i)

\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
is a vector subspace of
\mathcal{L}(E,L)
;

(ii)

\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L)
contains all finite rank operators with co-support in
\mathsf{span}(U^{\circ})
.

Proof 133

(i) Let
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
, and let
T,S\in\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
with constants
C_{V}^{T}
and
C_{V}^{S}
in (
17.2
). Then for every non-zero
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
, (
17.2
) is satisfied for the operator
\lambda T
with the constant
|\lambda|\cdot C_{V}^{T}
and hence
\lambda T\in\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
. Set
C_{V}:=C_{V}^{T}+C_{V}^{S}
. Then for every
n\in\omega
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, (
17.2
) implies

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})+S(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}+\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(S(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Therefore
T+S\in\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
, and hence
\prod_{q,p}^{U}(E,L)
is a vector space.

(ii) Since every finite rank operator
T
with co-support in
\mathsf{span}(U^{\circ})
is a finite sum of operators of the form
F(x):=\langle\eta,x\rangle\cdot y
, where
x\in E
and
\eta\in\mathsf{span}(U^{\circ})
, by (i), it suffices to check that
F\in\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L)
. To this end, let
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
and let
a>0
be such that
a\eta\in U^{\circ}
. Set
C_{V}:=\frac{q_{V}(y)}{a}
. Then for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(F(x_{i})\big)^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{V}(y)}{a}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle a\eta,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Thus
F\in\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L)
.∎

Remark 17.9

The condition “to have co-support in
\mathsf{span}(U^{\circ})
” in Proposition
17.8
is essential. Indeed, let
E=\mathbb{R}^{\omega}
,
L=\ell_{1}
,
U=[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{\omega{\setminus}\{0\}}
, and let
\eta=(0,1,0,\dots)\in\varphi=E^{\prime}
. We show that the operator
T=\langle\eta,\cdot\rangle y
, where
y
is a non-zero vector of
L
, does not belong to
\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L)
. It is easy to see that
U^{\circ}=[-1,1]\oplus\bigoplus_{\omega{\setminus}\{0\}}\{0\}
and hence
\eta\not\in\mathsf{span}(U^{\circ})
. Let
n=0
and
x_{0}:=(0,1,0,\dots)\in U
. Then
\|T(x_{0})\|=\|\langle\eta,x_{0}\rangle y\|=\|y\|\not=0
, but

\sup\big\{|\langle\chi,x_{0}\rangle|:\chi\in U^{\circ}\big\}=0.

Thus (
17.3
) is not satisfied and hence
T\not\in\prod_{p}^{U}(E,L)
.∎

The next result for Banach spaces is proved in Theorem 2.4 of
[
24
]
, we propose its direct proof.

Theorem 17.10
(Ideal Property)

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E_{0}
,
E
,
L
and
L_{0}
be locally convex spaces,
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
, and let
S:E_{0}\to E
,
T:E\to L
and
R:L\to L_{0}
be operators. If
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing, then
RTS
is
V
-
(q,p)
-summing, where
V=\big(S^{\ast}(U^{\circ})\big)^{\circ}
.

Proof 134

Since
U^{\circ}
is absolutely convex so is
S^{\ast}(U^{\circ})
. Therefore, by the Bipolar Theorem 8.3.8 of
[
67
]
,
V^{\circ}
is the weak
∗
closure of
S^{\ast}(U^{\circ})
, and hence for each
n\in\omega
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in V
, we have

\displaystyle\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\eta,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\eta\in V^{\circ}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\eta,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\eta\in S^{\ast}(U^{\circ})\Big\}

(17.7)

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,S(x_{i})\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Observe that if
x\in V
and
\chi\in U^{\circ}
, then
\eta=S^{\ast}(\chi)\in V^{\circ}
and

|\langle\chi,S(x)\rangle|=|\langle\eta,x\rangle|\leq 1

which means that
S(V)\subseteq U^{\circ\circ}=U
.

Fix an arbitrary
W_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L_{0})
and set
W:=R^{-1}(W_{0})
. Then
W\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
, and for every
y\in L
, we have

q_{W}(y):=\inf\{\lambda>0:y\in\lambda W\}=\inf\{\lambda>0:R(y)\in\lambda W_{0}\}=q_{W_{0}}R(y)

and hence
q_{W}=q_{W_{0}}R
. Therefore, for each
n\in\omega
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in V
, the
U
-
(q,p)
-summability of
T
, applied to
W
, implies

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{W_{0}}\big(RTS(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{W}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,S(x_{i})\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Hence, by (
17.7
), for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in V
, we obtain

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{W_{0}}\big(RTS(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{W}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\eta,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\eta\in V^{\circ}\Big\}

which means that
RTS
is
V
-
(q,p)
-summing.∎

The proof of the next result is similar to the proof of Theorem 10.4 of
[
24
]
.

Theorem 17.11
(Inclusion Property)

Let
1\leq p_{j}\leq q_{j}<\infty
(
j=1,2)
satisfy

p_{1}\leq p_{2},\;\;q_{1}\leq q_{2},\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{p_{2}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}.

Let
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces, and let
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
. Then
\prod_{q_{1},p_{1}}^{U}(E,L)\subseteq\prod_{q_{2},p_{2}}^{U}(E,L)
.

Proof 135

Let
T\in\prod_{q_{1},p_{1}}^{U}(E,L)
, and let
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
. Take
n\in\omega
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, and consider the next two cases.

Case 1. Assume that
p_{1}=p_{2}=p
. Then the inequalities
q_{1}\leq q_{2}
and
\|x\|_{\ell_{q_{2}}}\leq\|x\|_{\ell_{q_{1}}}
(
x\in\ell_{q_{1}}
) imply

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}}\leq\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{1}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Thus
T\in\prod_{q_{2},p_{2}}^{U}(E,L)
.

Case 2. Assume that
p_{1}<p_{2}
. Then the inequality
\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{p_{2}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}
implies
q_{1}<q_{2}
. Therefore we can define numbers
1<p,q<\infty
by

\tfrac{1}{p}:=\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{p_{2}}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;\;\tfrac{1}{q}:=\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}.

For every
i\leq n
, set
\lambda_{i}:=q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}/q}
, so that
q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}=q_{V}\big(T(\lambda_{i}x_{i})\big)^{q_{1}}
. Set
\lambda=\lambda_{0}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}
. Since the case
\lambda=0
is trivial, we assume that
\lambda>0
. Then
\tfrac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda}x_{i}\in U
because
U
is absolutely convex. Since
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing, we obtain

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}\Big)^{1/q_{1}}=\lambda\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(\tfrac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda}x_{i}\big)^{q_{1}}\Big)^{1/q_{1}}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{p_{1}}\cdot|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p_{1}}\Big)^{1/p_{1}}.

Since
q_{2}>q_{1}
, we apply Hölder’s inequality to the conjugate numbers
\tfrac{q}{p_{1}}
and
\tfrac{q}{q-p_{1}}
and
a_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{q_{1}}
and
b_{i}=|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{q_{1}}
in the right hand side to get

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{p_{1}\cdot\tfrac{q}{p_{1}}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}\cdot\tfrac{p_{1}}{q}}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p_{1}\cdot\tfrac{q}{q-p_{1}}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}\cdot\tfrac{q-p_{1}}{q}}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

\displaystyle=C_{V}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{\tfrac{q_{2}}{q}\cdot q}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{q}}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{\tfrac{p_{1}q}{q-p_{1}}}\Big)^{\tfrac{q-p_{1}}{p_{1}q}}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

\displaystyle=C_{V}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}\Big)^{\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{\tfrac{p_{1}q}{q-p_{1}}}\Big)^{\tfrac{q-p_{1}}{p_{1}q}}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Dividing both sides by the second factor of the right hand side and taking into account that

\tfrac{q-p_{1}}{p_{1}q}=\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q}=\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{q_{2}}\leq\tfrac{1}{p_{2}}

and hence
\tfrac{p_{1}q}{q-p_{1}}\geq p_{2}
, we obtain

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q_{2}}\Big)^{1/q_{2}}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p_{2}}\Big)^{1/p_{2}}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

which means that
T
is
U
-
(q_{2},p_{2})
-summing.∎

The next theorem extends Theorem 2.8 and Proposition 10.2 of
[
24
]
.

Theorem 17.12
(Injectivity of
\prod_{q,p}
)

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
,
L
and
L_{0}
be locally convex spaces, and let
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
. If
I:L\to L_{0}
is an embedding, then an operator
T:E\to L
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing if and only if so is
I\circ T:E\to L_{0}
.

Proof 136

The necessity follows from the ideal property Theorem
17.10
applied to
E_{0}=E
and
S=\mathsf{id}_{E}
. To prove the sufficiency, assume that
I\circ T:E\to L_{0}
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing. Fix an arbitrary
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
. Since
I
is an embedding, there is
W\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L_{0})
such that
W\cap I(L)=I(V)
. Since
I\circ T:E\to L_{0}
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing, there is
C_{W}>0
such that for every
n\in\omega
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{W}\big(I\circ T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{W}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\},

Observe that for every
y\in L
, we have

q_{V}(y)=\inf\{\lambda>0:y\in\lambda V\}=\inf\{\lambda>0:I(y)\in\lambda W\}=q_{W}\big(I(y)\big).

Therefore for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we obtain

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}=\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{W}\big(I\circ T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{W}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Thus
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing.∎

Let
H
be a normed space, and let
E
be a locally convex space. For every absolutely convex closed subset
U
of
E
, we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{U}(H,E)
the family of all operators
S:H\to E
such that
S(B_{H})\subseteq U
.
The next theorem generalizes Proposition 2.7 of
[
24
]
.

Theorem 17.13

Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
,
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces,
U
be an absolutely convex closed subset of
E
, and let
T:E\to L
be an operator. Denote
B_{\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0}}

(
or
B_{c_{0}^{0}}
if
p=1
)
by
B
.
If
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing, then for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
, there is
C_{V}>0
such that
T\circ S
is
B
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{B,V}(T\circ S)\leq C_{V}
for each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)

(
or for each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
)
. The converse is true if
T(U)
is a bounded subset of
L
.

Proof 137

Assume that
T
is a
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operator. Fix an arbitrary
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)
(or
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
). We show that for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
,
T\circ S
is
B
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{B,V}(T)\leq C_{V}
, where the constant
C_{V}
is defined by
T
. To this end, fix
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
and let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in B
. Recall that
S(B)\subseteq U
and observe also that for every
\chi\in U^{\circ}
and each
x\in B
, we have

|\langle S^{\ast}(\chi),x\rangle|=|\langle\chi,S(x)\rangle|\leq 1

which means that
S^{\ast}(U^{\circ})\subseteq B^{\circ}=B_{\ell_{p}}
. Then the
U
-
(q,p)
-summability of
T
, applied to
S(x_{0}),\dots,S(x_{n})\in U
, implies

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(TS(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T\big(S(x_{i})\big)\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,S(x_{i})\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle S^{\ast}(\chi),x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\eta,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\eta\in B^{\circ}\Big\}.

Thus
T\circ S
is
B
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{B,V}(T)\leq C_{V}
.

Conversely, assume that
T(U)
is a bounded subset of
L
and for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
, there is
C_{V}>0
such that
T\circ S
is
B
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{B,V}(T\circ S)\leq C_{V}
for each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)
(or for each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
). We show that
T
is
U
-
(q,p)
-summing. To this end, fix
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
and an arbitrary finite family
F=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq U
. Set

K:=\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}\;\;\big(\leq(n+1)^{1/p}\big)

and consider the following two possible cases.

Case 1. Assume that
K=0
. Then
\mathsf{span}(F)\subseteq U^{\circ\circ}=U
. Since
T(U)
is a bounded subset of
L
it follows that
F\subseteq\ker(T)
. Therefore

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}=0=C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}

for every
C_{V}>0
.

Case 2. Assume that
K>0
. Define a finite-rank operator
S\in\mathcal{L}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)
(or
S\in\mathcal{L}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
) by

S\big(a_{0}e_{0}^{\ast}+\cdots+a_{n}e_{n}^{\ast}+a_{n+1}e_{n+1}^{\ast}+\cdots\big):=\tfrac{1}{K}\big(a_{0}x_{0}+\cdots+a_{n}x_{n}\big),

where
(a_{n})\in\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0}
(or
\in c_{0}^{0}
if
p=1
). Since
S
has finite rank it is continuous.

We claim that
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)
(or
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
). Indeed, for every
\chi\in U^{\circ}
and each
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}\in B_{\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0}}
(or
\in B_{c_{0}^{0}}
if
p=1
), the Hölder inequality for
p>1
implies

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle\chi,S\big(a_{i}\big)_{i\in\omega}\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{K}\Big|\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle\Big|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|a_{i}|^{p^{\ast}}\Big)^{1/p^{\ast}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\leq 1

and, for
p=1
, we have

\Big|\big\langle\chi,S\big(a_{i}\big)_{i\in\omega}\big\rangle\Big|=\tfrac{1}{K}\Big|\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle\Big|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\cdot\sup_{i\in\omega}|a_{i}|\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|\leq\tfrac{1}{K}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|\leq 1.

Therefore
S\big(B\big)\subseteq U^{\circ\circ}=U
, and hence
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(\ell_{p^{\ast}}^{0},E)
(or
S\in\mathcal{L}_{U}(c_{0}^{0},E)
if
p=1
). This proves the claim.

By assumption the operator
T\circ S
is
B
-
(q,p)
-summing with
\pi_{q,p}^{B,V}(T\circ S)\leq C_{V}
. This means that (recall that
B^{\circ}=B_{\ell_{p}}
)

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}K^{q}\cdot q_{V}\big(TS(e_{i}^{\ast})\big)^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\leq K\cdot C_{V}\cdot\sup_{\eta\in B_{\ell_{p}}}\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\eta,e_{i}^{\ast}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq KC_{V}\cdot\sup\big\{\|\eta\|_{\ell_{p}}:\eta\in B_{\ell_{p}}\big\}=C_{V}\cdot K

\displaystyle=C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

Thus
T
is a
U
-
(q,p)
-summing operator, as desired.∎

It turns out that two Pietsch characterizations of
p
-summing operators hold true for every locally convex space
E
. To obtain desired extensions we should have the condition that
U^{\circ}
is weak
∗
compact. Taking into account that
U^{\circ}
is also absolutely convex, the Mackey–Arens theorem implies that
U
should be a neighborhood of zero in the Mackey topology. This explains the condition
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E_{\mu})
in the following generalization of the Pietsch Domination Theorem 2.12 of
[
24
]
.

Theorem 17.14
(Pietsch Domination Theorem)

Let
1\leq p<\infty
,
E
and
L
be locally convex spaces,
T:E\to L
be an operator,
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E_{\mu})
and let
K:=\big(U^{\circ},\sigma(E^{\prime},E){\restriction}_{U^{\circ}}\big)
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

T
is
U
-
p
-summing;

(ii)

for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
there exist a regular probability measure
\mu_{V}
on the compact space
K
and a constant
C_{V}>0
such that

q_{V}\big(T(x)\big)^{p}\leq C^{p}_{V}\cdot\int_{K}|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}d\mu_{V}(\chi)\;\;\mbox{ for every $x\in E$}.

Proof 138

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) In the proof we consider
C(K)
over
\mathbb{R}
. By the definition of
U
-
p
-summing operators, for every
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
there is a constant
C_{V}>0
such that for every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\leq C_{V}\cdot\sup\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}:\chi\in U^{\circ}\Big\}.

(17.8)

Using the constant
C_{V}
from this inequality, for any finite subset
M
of
E
, we define a continuous function
f_{M}:K\to\mathbb{R}
by

f_{M}(\chi):=\sum_{x\in M}\Big(q_{V}\big(T(x)\big)^{p}-C^{p}_{V}\cdot|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}\Big)\quad(\chi\in K).

Claim 1. The family
Q:=\{f_{M}:M\subseteq E\mbox{ is finite}\}\subseteq C(K)
is convex. Indeed, if
M
and
N
are finite subsets of
E
and
0<\lambda<1
, then
\lambda\cdot f_{M}+(1-\lambda)\cdot f_{N}=f_{S}
, where

S=\{\lambda^{1/p}x:x\in M{\setminus}N\}\cup\{(1-\lambda)^{1/p}x:x\in N{\setminus}M\}\cup(M\cap N).

This proves Claim 1.

Set
P:=\{f\in C(K):f(\chi)>0\mbox{ for every }\chi\in K\}
. Then:

(a)

P
is an open, convex and positive cone in
C(K)
;

(b)

Q\cap P=\emptyset
.

Indeed, (a) is clear. To check (b), let
f_{M}\in Q
. Since
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E_{\mu})
, the set
U
is absorbing. Therefore, by homogeneity, (
17.8
) is satisfied for all
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in E
. It follows from (
17.8
) that there is
\chi\in K
such that
f_{M}(\chi)\leq 0
. Thus
f_{M}\not\in P
, as desired.

Taking into account Claim 1 and (a)-(b), Proposition 2.13(ii) of
[
29
]
implies that there is a regular Borel measure
\mu_{V}\in C(K)^{\prime}
on
K
such that

\langle\mu_{V},g\rangle\leq c:=\sup\{\langle\mu_{V},h\rangle:h\in Q\}<\langle\mu_{V},f\rangle

(17.9)

for all
g\in Q
and
f\in P
.

Claim 2.
c=0
. Indeed, since
g_{\{0\}}=0\in Q
we obtain
c\geq 0
. On the other hand, since every positive constant function belongs to
P
, (
17.9
) implies
c\leq 0
. Thus
c=0
.

Since
\mu_{V}
is a continuous functional and, by Claim 2,
\langle\mu_{V},f\rangle>0
for every
f\in P
, it follows that
\langle\mu_{V},f\rangle\geq 0
for every
f\geq 0
in
C(K)
. Therefore
\mu_{V}
is a positive regular Borel measure on
K
. Dividing (
17.9
) by
\|\mu_{V}\|
we can assume that
\mu_{V}
is a probability measure. Since
c=0
we can apply (
17.9
) to the function
g_{\{x\}}\in Q
,
x\in E
, to obtain

\int_{K}\Big(q_{V}\big(T(x)\big)^{p}-C^{p}_{V}\cdot|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}\Big)d\mu_{V}(\chi)\leq 0,

or, since
\mu_{V}
is a probability measure,

q_{V}\big(T(x)\big)^{p}\leq C^{p}_{V}\cdot\int_{K}|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}d\mu_{V}(\chi).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) For each
V\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(L)
and every
n\in\omega
and each
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we apply (ii) to obtain

\sum_{i=0}^{n}q_{V}\big(T(x_{i})\big)^{p}\leq C^{p}_{V}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n}\int_{K}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}d\mu_{V}(\chi)\leq C^{p}_{V}\cdot\sup_{\chi\in U^{\circ}}\sum_{i=0}^{n}|\langle\chi,x_{i}\rangle|^{p}

and hence
T
is
U
-
p
-summing.∎

Let
K
be a compact space, and let
\mu
be a regular probability measure on
K
. Denote by
J_{p}
and
J_{p}^{\infty}
the identity inclusions of
C(K)
into
L_{p}(\mu)
and
L_{\infty}(\mu)
into
L_{p}(\mu)
, respectively. It is clear that
J_{p}
and
J_{p}^{\infty}
are continuous. If
L
is a Banach space, we denote by
i_{L}:L\to\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}})
the canonical isometric embedding. Now we prove an analogue of the Pietsch Factorization Theorem 2.13 of
[
24
]
.

Theorem 17.15
(Pietsch Factorization Theorem)

Let
1\leq p<\infty
, and let
T:E\to L
be an operator from a locally convex space
E
to a Banach space
L
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

T
is
U
-
p
-summing for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
;

(ii)

there exist
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, a regular probability measure
\mu
on the compact space
K:=\big(U^{\circ},\sigma(E^{\prime},E){\restriction}_{U^{\circ}}\big)
, an operator
I_{U}:E\to C(K)
, a closed subspace
H_{p}
of
L_{p}(\mu)
and an operator
{\hat{T}}:H_{p}\to L
such that the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{I_{E}}
\textstyle{L}
\textstyle{I_{U}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{p}^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}
\textstyle{H_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{T}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}_{H_{p}}}
\textstyle{C(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{p}}
\textstyle{L_{p}(\mu)}

in which
I_{E}
is the co-restriction of
I_{U}
to
I_{U}(E)
and
\mathsf{id}
and
\mathsf{id}_{H_{p}}
are the corresponding identity inclusions of
I_{U}(E)
into
C(K)
and
H_{p}
into
L_{p}(\mu)
.

(iii)

there exist
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, a regular probability measure
\mu
on the compact space
K:=\big(U^{\circ},\sigma(E^{\prime},E){\restriction}_{U^{\circ}}\big)
, an operator
I_{U}:E\to C(K)
, and an operator
{\tilde{T}}:L_{p}(\mu)\to\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}})
such that the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{I_{U}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{L}}
\textstyle{\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{C(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{p}}
\textstyle{L_{p}(\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\tilde{T}}}

(iv)

there exist
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
, a regular probability measure
\mu
on the compact space
K:=\big(U^{\circ},\sigma(E^{\prime},E){\restriction}_{U^{\circ}}\big)
, an operator
I_{U}^{\infty}:E\to L_{\infty}(\mu)
, and an operator
{\tilde{T}}:L_{p}(\mu)\to\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}})
such that the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{I_{U}^{\infty}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{L}}
\textstyle{\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}}).}
\textstyle{L_{\infty}(\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{p}^{\infty}}
\textstyle{L_{p}(\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\tilde{T}}}

Proof 139

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) Assume that
T
is a
U
-
p
-summing operator for some
U\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{c}(E)
. Then, by the Pietsch Domination Theorem
17.14
, there exist a regular probability measure
\mu
on the compact space
K:=\big(U^{\circ},\sigma(E^{\prime},E){\restriction}_{U^{\circ}}\big)
and a constant
C>0
such that

\|T(x)\|^{p}\leq C^{p}\cdot\int_{K}|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}d\mu(\chi)\;\;\mbox{ for every $x\in E$}.

(17.10)

Since every
x\in E
is a continuous function on
K
, the canonical inclusion
I_{U}:E\to C(K)
,
I_{U}(x)(\chi):=\langle\chi,x\rangle
, is well-defined. As
|I_{U}(x)(\chi)|\leq 1
for every
x\in U
and each
\chi\in K
, it follows that
I_{U}(U)\subseteq B_{C(K)}
and hence
I_{U}
is continuous. Denote by
I_{E}
the co-restriction of
I_{U}
onto the image
I_{U}(E)
, and let
\mathsf{id}:I_{U}(E)\to C(K)
be the identity inclusion.

Set
Y:=J_{p}\big(I_{U}(E)\big)\subseteq L_{p}(\mu)
and define a map
R:Y\to L
by

R(y):=T(x),\;\;\;\mbox{ where $y=J_{p}(I_{U}(x))$.}

The map
R
is well-defined because if
y=J_{p}(I_{U}(x))=J_{p}(I_{U}(x^{\prime}))
for some
x^{\prime}\in E
, then, by the injectivity of
J_{p}
,
I_{U}(x)=I_{U}(x^{\prime})
and hence
\langle\chi,x-x^{\prime}\rangle=0
for every
\chi\in U^{\circ}
. In particular,
\mathsf{span}(x-x^{\prime})\subseteq U^{\circ\circ}=U
. Since, by (
17.10
),
T(U)\subseteq C\cdot B_{L}
and
B_{L}
does not contain linear subspaces, we obtain
\mathsf{span}(x-x^{\prime})\subseteq\ker(T)
and hence
T(x)=T(x^{\prime})
. Thus
R
is well-defined.

To show that
R
is continuous, let
y=J_{p}(I_{U}(x))\in Y
be such that

\|y\|_{L_{p}(\mu)}^{p}:=\int_{K}|\langle\chi,x\rangle|^{p}d\mu(\chi)\leq 1.

Then, by (
17.10
), we obtain
\|R(y)\|=\|T(x)\|\leq C
. Thus
R
is continuous.

Denote by
H_{p}
the completion of
Y
in the Banach space
L_{p}(\mu)
, and let
{\hat{T}}
be the extension of
R
from
Y
to
H_{p}
. Then, by construction, we have
T={\hat{T}}\circ J_{p}^{E}\circ I_{E}
, as desired.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) Since the Banach space
\ell_{\infty}(B_{L^{\prime}})
